- An interrupted walk resumes: after every page the adapter tells `sync` where the walk could continue, and `sync` writes that checkpoint (cursor, assets so far, the boundary ids, the watermark so far) under `walk` in `state/immich.json` at every checkpoint of the record, never ahead of the lines on disk; the next run says `resuming where the last run stopped, at x of N assets` and asks for nothing before the saved cursor. The walk's watermark is written only once the walk completes and is never later than the moment the walk began, so what the server changed meanwhile is pulled next time. `--restart` walks the whole library again; `--since` begins a new walk; a dry run resumes too and saves nothing.
- `logbook sync --all` runs the quick sources first and `immich` last, and its first line says the order (`running dawarich, imessage, …, then immich last`), so a photo walk of hours never holds up the messages, the calendar, the positions or the weather. `--all` refuses `--restart` as it refuses `--since`.
- `logbook add <mbox> --dry-run` counts a message that appears twice in the same mbox (Takeout writes one under every label it carries) as the real run does — written once, the repeat refused — and says `1 repeated in the input` beside what is already in the record, instead of counting both as lines it would add. The counts of `sync`'s summary line carry thousands separators.
- One writer at a time (#234): `append` and `append_many`, and `add`, `sync` and `import backup` for
  the whole of their run, hold `state/writer.lock` in the record folder, so a `sync`'s walk is inside
  it. A second writer prints `another logbook command is writing to this record (<command>, since
  <time>); waiting` and waits, or with the global `--no-wait` refuses with exit 2; a lock whose
  process is gone is taken over with one line saying so; a reader never takes it. The guard behind
  the courtesy: before every batch is written, `append_many` reads `logbook.json` again and refuses
  (`HeadMoved`, one sentence, exit 2, nothing written) when the head moved since the batch was
  computed, so a batch chained from a stale head can never reach the files. A long `sync` whose
  head another command had moved past used to lose its batch with `sqlite3.IntegrityError: UNIQUE
  constraint failed: lines.seq` from the index, after the month files had taken it.

### Added
- `logbook serve mcp` gains three read tools behind the same `mcp` ceiling of `policy/crossing.json` as the
  nine before (`docs/mcp.md`): `day_lines(day?, kinds?)` is `logbook show DAY` as one object per standing
  line, in time order, with the line's id, kind, profile, local time, counterpart and the text part of the
  row `show` prints, `kinds` keeping only the profile families asked for (`mail`, `message`, `transcript`,
  `note`, `location`, `photo`, `calendar`); `line(id)` is one line in full, the row, the payload and the
  text, a transcript's turns read from the attachment store as `promises` reads them; `digest(period?,
  date?)` is the text `logbook digest DATE` prints, or the seven digests of the ISO week that holds the
  date. Lines above the ceiling are left out of `day_lines` and counted in `above_ceiling` with nothing of
  them crossing, not even an id; `line` refuses one naming its tier and nothing else; the digest is read
  through the gate, so at a ceiling of 1 it is the digest of a record that holds only tier 1. An agent on
  this machine could search a day's lines but not read them without a word to search for, read a
  transcript's summary but not its text, and not get the digest; now it can, under the ceiling. The server
  logs one line per call on the `logbook.mcp` logger (stderr): the tool, the day or id, and counts — never
  content. The row `show` prints for a line moved from `logbook/commands/rows.py` to
  `logbook/contrib/rows.py` so a contrib reader can print it (the commands import the same names); nothing
  any command prints changed.
- `logbook people review` (`logbook/contrib/people_review.py`, `docs/people.md`): the record's people in two
  layers. An **observed** person is what one source saw, keyed by its strongest identifier — the email
  address, else the phone number in E.164 (a WhatsApp JID read as the number it spells), else the folded
  name — never by a per-observation id, so an address seen under three display names is one person; they
  are a derived table in the index, `observed_people`, built from the standing mail, message, transcript
  and event lines, stamped with the head and rebuilt when the record grows, nothing stored in the record. A
  **canonical** person is one the resolution lines name, as `people` lists them. The command proposes each
  observed person not yet placed against the canonical people, ranked by the evidence (same email 60, same
  phone 50, same name 20 and +5 per further source, together on the same days 2 per day) and numbered;
  `--accept N[,N]` writes the alias line `people merge` writes, one per identifier as the lines wrote it;
  `--reject N[,N]` writes one `people-review/v1` line (RFC 0035, draft) saying the two are not the same
  person, so the pair never comes back; `--all-above SCORE` accepts at or above. Nothing is promoted on its
  own; every number is checked before the first line is written; `--json` carries the queue, the unplaced
  and a stable id per pair.
- `logbook people --priority [--year YYYY]` ranks the canonical people heard in the last year by a plain
  sum printed beside each name: 3 per day together, 1 per message exchanged (the smaller direction, up to
  50), 2 per meeting, 10 when the last real contact is within 30 days and 5 within 90. No model.
- `logbook promises` reads mail and messages (`docs/promises.md`): every `mail/v1` and `message/v1` line beside the transcripts and the notes, by rules alone, in two sections. **I promised** is every commitment cue in a line you wrote (a mail from one of your addresses, a message with `from_me`, your turn of a transcript, a note); **I was asked** is every request cue in a line you received. Who wrote the line decides, from `owner_emails`, `policy/owner.json` and the resolution lines, never the words: a request in your own mail is you asking and is not proposed, a commitment in someone else's is theirs and `--all` lists it apart. Each row carries the day, the counterpart (`to Ola Nordmann`, `by Kari Nordmann`: the person the record resolves behind the address, the number or the chat), the sentence, the due hint, the proposal's id for `promises done` and the origin line's id, which `logbook day sign DAY --confirm` takes (RFC 0034). New flags `--mine`, `--theirs` and `--source mail|message|transcript|note`; `--since` as before. The cue list, documented in full, gains the terse forms of both languages (`will do`, `on it`, `mach ich`, `schick ich dir`, `erledige ich`, `versprochen`, a short `by Friday` or `bis Freitag` on its own) and the requests (`can you`, `could you send`, `would you mind`, `please confirm`, `let me know`, `don't forget to`, `kannst du`, `könnten Sie`, `schick mir`, `sag mir Bescheid`, `vergiss nicht`). A mail body is read up to its first reply header or signature delimiter with the quoted lines left out, so a reply that quotes a promise never proposes it again. The rules show by default; the judge stays opt-in (`--judge`), reads your commitments only (never a request), and a verdict of no, or below 0.6, sets a candidate aside, which `--all` still lists. Under `--json` every proposal carries `role`, `counterpart` and `line`; the report carries `sources`, `mine_only`, `theirs_only` and `all` where it carried `judged_only`. Recall test on a synthetic fortnight of the Oslo persona (24 planted commitments, 12 planted requests, 60 distractor lines across mail, iMessage, WhatsApp and one transcript; `tests/test_promises_wider_net.py`): the rules read 22 of 24 and 11 of 12 at precision 0.97; the test holds them to 20, 10 and 0.8.
- `logbook repair fork [--apply]` (`logbook/core/fork.py`, #234): a record whose month files hold two
  chains forked at one head — a long `sync` wrote its batch chained from a head another command had
  moved past, and the index refused it after the files took it — is diagnosed (the fork, each orphan
  chain's count, seq range, sources and files, `logbook.json` against the record's chain; counts and
  hashes only, nothing written) and with `--apply` repaired: every orphan line is copied in order to
  `repair/<stamp>-removed.jsonl` in the record folder with a `repair/<stamp>-report.json`, each touched
  month file is written again without them with every kept line byte for byte, `logbook.json` is set
  to the record's head, the index rebuilt and `verify` run. The record's chain is the one the index
  agrees with, else the longer one, else the command refuses; `--keep-head <hash>` (a full head as
  the dry run prints every chain's, or a unique prefix) names it and overrides both rules, and
  without an index the dry run says it is the safer choice; it also refuses a break that is not a
  fork, a torn month file, a sealed orphan line it cannot open, and a folder under a sync client.
  `doctor`'s record check names it beside `logbook verify`; `docs/recovery.md` is the page.
- `logbook day sign YYYY-MM-DD` takes `--confirm none` and `--at RFC3339`, RFC 0034 amendment 2 (`docs/rfcs/0034-the-signed-day.md`, `docs/signed-day.md`). `--confirm none` signs a day with nothing confirmed: the `signed-day/v1` line carries an empty `confirmed`, the page digest is still over the page as shown, the header says `signed` and the day's lines cross as those of any signed day; the word goes alone, and `none` beside an id or with `--kept`, `--missed`, `--dropped` or `--carried` is refused in one sentence. `--at` is the moment the owner clicked rather than the moment the command ran, an RFC 3339 moment with an offset or `Z`, stored in UTC and shown in the header in local time; a malformed moment (refused with an example), one more than five minutes ahead of this clock, or one before the day signed starts in the record's zone is refused and nothing is written. Without either flag nothing changes.
- Dispositions on the signed day, RFC 0034 amendment 1 (`docs/rfcs/0034-the-signed-day.md`, `docs/signed-day.md`): `logbook day sign YYYY-MM-DD` gains `--kept ID,ID`, `--missed ID,ID`, `--dropped ID,ID` and `--carried ID,ID`, and the `signed-day/v1` line an optional `dispositions` object, a confirmed line's id → one of the four words; a line given one is confirmed by that, and an unknown id, a retracted line, a line given twice or a flag that names no line is refused in a sentence. `day DAY` prints ✓, ✗, – or → before each line the signature disposed of and carries the map under `signed.dispositions`; `show` counts them on the signature's row; `promises` treats kept and dropped as closed, missed as closed and flagged (`missed on <day>`), carried as still open, and carries `disposition` in its JSON; `export crossing` carries the field with the line. The field is optional: every signature written before, the conformance samples' 32nd line included, is valid and its hash unchanged.
- The signed day, RFC 0034 (`docs/rfcs/0034-the-signed-day.md`, `docs/signed-day.md`, `logbook/core/signing.py`): `logbook day sign YYYY-MM-DD [--note TEXT] [--confirm ID,ID]` appends one `signed-day/v1` line, tier 1, source `manual`, saying the owner read the day's page and these are its facts — the day, the owner as subject, the ids confirmed (every line on the page by default, else the seqs or ids named), the digest of the page as shown (the SHA-256 over the hashes of the lines the page listed, in order, so any implementation recomputes it) and an optional one-line note. Nothing is rewritten; a day signed again is superseded by the later line, and a retracted signature leaves the earlier one standing. Only the owner signs: the MCP server has no such tool. `show DAY` says `signed <when>` or `unsigned` in its header and lists a signature as a row on the day it was written; `day DAY` says the same after the weekday, `, the page has changed since` when the day grew after signing, and carries it under `signed` in `--json`. The conformance sample gains a 32nd line, the first day signed on the last evening (new heads in `conformance/README.md`).
- `logbook export crossing` crosses the lines of signed days only, by default (RFC 0034 rule 5): a line's day is the local day of its `at`, a signature crosses with the day it signs, the console says how many lines were held back on unsigned days, and `--unsigned` crosses them too. The manifest's `policy` carries `signed_days` (`only` or `any`), `counts` gains `held_back_unsigned`, and the `crossing/v1` line's `policy` gains `signed_only`. The shared page, the trip bundle, the vault and the site keep their own selections.
- Readiness (`logbook/core/readiness.py`): `logbook day DAY` gains a `readiness` row, and `--json` a `readiness` block, saying for each class of source — mail, message, meeting, location, photo, calendar — whether the day has lines of it, from which sources, and which usual sources (a line of the class on four in five of the logged days of the last four weeks, not disabled in `policy/import.json`) have not delivered; from the record and the policy alone, no connection opened, nothing written.
- ADR 0022, the record's home (`docs/adr/0022-the-records-home.md`): one machine holds the only
  writable copy, runs the sync schedule, the MCP server and the local models, and serves the Day page
  over the owner's own network; every other device is a reader; an orchestrator elsewhere reaches the
  record only through the crossing; sources come to the home; a collaborator is an OS user on it; and
  every cloud model or service that may see record content has its own entry in `policy/crossing.json`.
  `logbook sync --install-schedule` names this machine the home in `state/home.json` (hostname and
  date; a second install on the same host keeps the date). `doctor` gains a `home` line (pass on the
  home or when none is set, a warn elsewhere: writers belong on the home) and a `crossing` line listing
  every destination's ceiling as it counts today.
- `policy/crossing.json`: an entry may carry `"until": "YYYY-MM-DD"`, the last day it holds; from the
  next day it counts as `max_tier` 0 for `export crossing`, `export vault`, `export site` and the MCP
  server, the refusal says when it expired, and `doctor` names it. An `until` that is not a date is
  refused naming the file.

## 0.6.0 — 2026-10-06

Twenty-two commands where there were forty-nine, and `logbook --help` on one screen; every old name still works and says the new one. One package in three tiers, core, contrib and labs, with a thin command line that loads no adapter until one is asked for. Tiers 2 and 3 sealed at rest with age (SPEC v0.3, RFC 0029): the chain still covers the real payload, so a keyless `verify` holds. Property tests of the format, the chain, the tiers, time and identity, and a strict reader that reports the last whole line of a torn month file. The profile freeze (RFC 0031): nineteen profiles frozen with one conformance fixture each, two withdrawn, `trip/v1` written as `journey/v1`. Mail at scale, read through the index. The paper products: a year on one sheet, a week on four pages, a trip page that prints. Local models for transcripts and photo descriptions, nothing leaving the machine. The manifesto, the rules, the open problems, governance and the roadmap, with the first letter to builders. A synthetic forty-year record published with the docs, and `logbook demo` for trying every command on nothing of yours. Issues closed on the way: #83, #96, #98, #118, #121, #122, and tonight #79 (`add` says what the record already held) and #123 (`show` orders a day by the instant).

**Breaking, and the migration.** The old command names (`year`, `trips`, `infer`, `import-backup`, `person`, …) still run, print one line naming the new command, and will be removed in 0.7. The 0.5 import paths (`logbook.store`, `logbook.adapters.*`, …) work through shims with a DeprecationWarning until 0.7; `docs/migration-0.6.md` lists every move. A format 0.2 record is read unchanged and never migrated; it becomes 0.3 only when it is given recipients and sealed.

### Added
- Reading never opens a connection, now a test (`tests/test_no_network.py`) and a paragraph of `SECURITY.md`: the `no_network` fixture of `tests/conftest.py` replaces the socket, `urllib`, `http.client` and, where installed, the `httpx`, `requests` and `websockets` request paths with ones that record and refuse every connection; `day`, `days`, `show`, `trips`, `trip`, `rollup` (every kind), `derive stays`, `year`, `digest`, `stats`, `ledger`, `keepers`, `places list`, `people`, `assets status`, `sources --gaps`, `search` and `verify`, each in its text and `--json` forms, run against the demo record under it, as do every page of `serve` through its `Site` and `mcp --inspect`, and the list of attempts must be empty afterwards, whatever the command printed or caught. The same run holds `verify` on `conformance/sample-logbook` to the head `conformance/README.md` documents. No reader attempted a connection; no code changed.
- `apple-podcasts` from an iPhone backup, with `completed` and the feed's transcript URL (`docs/adapters/listen.md`): `import-backup --only podcasts` is a new source — the Podcasts library, `Documents/MTLibrary.sqlite` under the app group `group.com.apple.podcasts`, is copied into `inbox/ios-backup-<udid>/apple-podcasts/` with its `-wal`/`-shm` and read by the same adapter as a copy or the Mac's own store, so the phone and the Mac dedupe each other. Every `listen/v1` line now says `completed`: the playhead within 30 s of the duration, or rewound to zero with a play counted, as the app leaves an episode it played to the end; absent when the store has no playhead column, false when it cannot show either, never a guess (RFC 0019 gains the field; rule 3 reads it first). Where the store keeps the URL of the transcript the episode's feed publishes (`<podcast:transcript>`; the columns are discovered by PRAGMA, none required) it lands under `extra.transcript_url` — the URL alone, never fetched: a reader never touches the network, and Apple's own transcript identifiers, which are not public, are never recorded. Fetching them, as an explicit `logbook fetch transcripts`, is the follow-up noted in the docs and not written. `rollup listen` gains a **podcasts** section per year: the episodes' hours by show (as the services spell them, by hours then episodes) and by month, printed only for a year with an episode; under `--json` as `podcasts` with `by_show`, `months` and the line ids. Synthetic fixtures only.
- `logbook rollup people --drifting [--until YYYY-MM-DD] [--window 365] [--min-contacts 3] [--json]` (`logbook/drifting.py`): for every person the record resolves, the contacts in the recent window — `--window` days ending on `--until`, the record's last day by default — against the same number of days before it, and the people whose frequency fell most, the larger share lost first. A contact is a direct message either way, a call answered or not, a calendar entry attended, or a stay the with module confirms them at (`together`); mail and a tagged face are not one. Per person the two counts by channel, the fall, the days from the last real contact to the window's last day, the channels that went quiet and the last shared place; under `--json` every number carries its line ids. A person with fewer than `--min-contacts` contacts in the earlier window is not listed. Two readings of `logbook people`'s reader, one per window, compared and never written; it says what the record holds and suggests nothing. Documented in `docs/rollups.md` with the method and its blind spots (a person met in person daily with nothing logged about it looks drifting; a source not brought up to date reads as silence; a record younger than the two windows undercounts the earlier one, and the rollup warns). The people reader's `Channel` keeps the ids of its lines (`ids`), not in its JSON; nothing else changes. Synthetic fixture: two fortnights of the Oslo persona's circle.
- The digest's closing question comes from the record (`logbook/questions.py`, RFC 0027, `docs/rfcs/0027-reflection.md`): `policy/questions.json` lists the questions — `id`, `text` (English, optional `text_de`), `kind` (gratitude, savouring, people, place, body, open), `when` (the day's facts that must hold: `travelled`, `aboard`, `night_away`, `reunion` for someone seen after ninety days, `!photos`, `long_sleep`, ...; the table is in the RFC), `weight` and `source` (a free-text citation, empty in the defaults) — written with its defaults the first time and never overwritten. `logbook digest` reads the day's facts from the parts it already has, draws one of the questions whose conditions hold by weight (a `{proposed}`, `{missing}`, `{promise}` or `{event}` in the text filled from the day), never the same id two days running, and remembers the last seven in `state/questions.json` — bookkeeping beside the record, never a line; a day's question stands when the day is read again. The defaults are the seven nudges the digest used to choose in code, each as a question with its condition, plus three placeholders; the content of the bank is explicitly left to a future author (the RFC's *The question bank — to be written*). `logbook questions list|add|disable` manages the file; a bank that is not the documented shape stops the digest naming the file. Under `--json` the digest's `question` is `{id, kind, text, facts}` where it was `{about, text}`. Tests for the facts, the weighted choice and the no-repeat rule (`tests/test_questions.py`).
- `logbook setup` (`logbook/setup.py`): the guided first run, for someone who has never used a terminal. Six steps, one question at a time, each with its default in brackets and a *Why:* line: where to keep the record (a folder iCloud Drive, Dropbox, OneDrive or Google Drive syncs is refused with the reason, `doctor.cloud_folder`, and so is a code checkout; a record already there is kept), the timezone (validated against this machine's zone database, never swapped), who you are (names, emails and phones into `policy/owner.json`, added to what it lists, the emails as `owner_emails` in `logbook.json` too, so you are never your own company), your home place (coordinates pasted from a maps app, never an address: one entry of kind `home` in `places.json` and the naming as one `note/v1` line, as `places name` writes it), what is on this machine (a Google Takeout under `~/Downloads`, each product an adapter reads listed and the ones `policy/import.json` switches off said, a zip named and left to unzip; an iPhone backup under MobileSync, always imported with `--only` the stores found, never bare; an encrypted one names `LOGBOOK_BACKUP_PASSWORD` and `read -s` and is never asked for the password; Messages on a Mac through `sync imessage`; each offered with a dry run first and its counts shown before anything is written; then the live sources by the names of their variables and where each key comes from, never a value), and `doctor`. Enter takes the default, `s` skips the step, `q` or the end of the input stops; the steps answered are kept in `state/setup.json` (bookkeeping beside the record, like every file under `state/`) and `logbook setup` again continues where it stopped; `--step NAME` runs one step again, `--again` every step, `--yes` takes every default and asks nothing (tests, scripts). Nothing printed spells the home directory (`~/…`), nothing printed or asked for is a secret. `Logbook.set_timezone` and `Logbook.set_owner_emails` (`logbook/store.py`) are the two settings it writes to `logbook.json`. `docs/first-hour.md` is the human companion: what to expect in the first hour, with the screens the wizard shows, in the docs nav; the README points at both.
- Four more Google Takeout products, each its own sub-adapter in `logbook/adapters/takeout/` and sniffed by `logbook add` from its folder, each with a docs page under *Adapters* ([Tasks](docs/adapters/takeout-tasks.md), [Maps](docs/adapters/takeout-maps.md), [Home App](docs/adapters/takeout-home.md), [YouTube](docs/adapters/takeout-youtube.md)): **`google-takeout-maps`** (`Maps (your places)/`): `Reviews.json`, in both spellings Google has used, becomes one `highlight/v1` line per review — the place as the title, the owner's words as the quote, stars and no words a bookmark of the place, the rating, Maps url, point and country under `extra`; `Saved Places.json` is never a line (a saved place is a name for coordinates, not an observation) but counted, and `logbook places propose --takeout PATH` feeds the saved places — name, coordinates, the list they were saved to, the day they were saved — as candidates: one within 300 m of an unnamed stay is shown under it and becomes the suggested name (`--write`, Enter adopts it), the rest are listed for `places add`; under `--json` each proposal carries `saved` and the object `saved_elsewhere`. **`google-takeout-home`** (`Home App/HomeHistory.json`): a member's arrival, a departure, the alarm armed (home or away) and disarmed are one `event/v1` line each at tier 1 (calendar `Google Home`, the home as `location`, the activity, device, member and words under `extra`), read by what the keys say so a renamed key still reads; sound events, alarm clips, other events and `SoundSensing/` and `SecurityAlarmClips/` are never read, counted where they are events. A Home line inside the owner's stay at home is evidence the Day attaches to the night's stay and that promotes a short cluster to a stay; it does not yet stand in for a track with no points (a Day rule, SPEC §3.2.3). **`google-takeout-youtube`** now reads Takeout's default HTML (`watch-history.html`, `search-history.html`, the outer-cell/content-cell cards, streamed through the standard library's parser) beside the JSON, with the same mapping; the HTML's clock in the owner's zone by abbreviation is read in UTC, or in the record's zone when that zone uses the abbreviation at that wall-clock time (`takeout.times.parse_in_zone`), else counted as an unknown zone and never guessed. **`google-takeout-tasks`** is unchanged and gets its page. The saved-places reader behind `places import-takeout` learns the day a place was saved and skips review features. Synthetic fixtures for all four.
- The paper edition: `logbook year YYYY --html PATH --print` and `logbook trip ID --html PATH --print` (`logbook/print_page.py`) write one HTML document designed for A4 and US Letter with CSS paged media — a cover (the owner's name from `policy/owner.json`, else a resolution's label, else the owner email; the year or the trip's dates), a contents page (the months with their days, nights away, flights and keepers, and the year's trips; or the days of the trip, then its route with the trip page's map on a page of its own), and one spread per month of the year or per day of the trip: the facts (the nights, the places by nights numbered to the map, the people confirmed — never a proposal — the flights, the sleep and the steps when the record has them, the keepers by lane) beside a map drawn as an inline SVG from the location lines themselves, the owner's and the asset's while aboard, with no tiles; then the keepers (RFC 0024) as hero photos sized to the page, one to a page, two stacked, up to six in a grid, more on another page, and no photo page when the span has none. Page numbers come from the page counter. Photos are referenced where they are, never copied: the attachment under the record when the pixels are stored (a §1.1 reference under `content` or `attachment`), else the original path the library wrote (`path`, `original_path`), else a frame with the photo's name. One inline stylesheet, no script, no link, nothing fetched. Every typographic constant — margins, text block, grid, type scale, faces, ink, map box, photo heights — is in `logbook/print_layout.py`, whose `stylesheet()` builds the CSS, so a designer rethemes without touching logic. Fixtures: the demo record's 2026 and its yacht week (`tests/fixtures/demo/print_*.html`, `scripts/make_print_fixtures.py`), pinned byte for byte and checked structurally (every tag closed in order, every `src` an `img`'s with `alt`, nothing from the network), and a day with no photos lays out. Docs: `docs/print.md`, with how to make the PDF in a browser or with headless Chromium. `--print` without `--html` exits 2.
- `story/v1`, RFC 0028 (`rfcs/0028-story-v1.md`, a draft left unfinished on purpose, with *Open questions for the author* for the non-technical co-author who will finish it): a *told* story, as distinct from an observed event — the thing told, `refers_to` (the time it is about, as told and as a span: a year, a month, a day, a decade, or a phrase such as `before the war` that is on no day), `told_at`, `teller` and `listener` as RFC 0006 refs with the label known at the time, `confidence` as the teller states it (`sure`, `fuzzy`, `disputed`), `source` (conversation, voice memo, interview, letter) and the transcript attachment it came from. Tier 2 by default: it carries someone else's words. The RFC says why a story is on the day it was told and never on the day it refers to, and how `show person` and a future `year --print` may list stories apart. `logbook add story <file>... --teller <ref> --listener <ref> [--refers-to 1961|1961-05|1961-05-04|1950s|<words>] [--confidence sure|fuzzy|disputed] [--at …] [--tier 2|3]` (`logbook/story.py`) writes one line per file from a text or Markdown file (a front-matter block may carry the same fields; a flag wins) or a transcript/v1 JSON (the text is the transcript's, its bytes go to the attachment store, `transcript_line` names the transcript/v1 line when the record has it); a ref is `kind:value`, an address, a `+` number or a name the record's resolution lines make one person; `raw_id` is `story:<sha256 of the file>`, so a file added twice is one line; tier 1 is refused. `show` prints a story on its told day as `📖 refers to <when> — told by <name> to <name>` (`--raw`: the refs). `day <date>` lists the standing stories whose `refers_to` covers the date under *Stories about this day*, after what is unplaced and never in the timeline, each with its confidence, told day and first line; `--json` carries them under `stories`. Story text is in the search index. Fixtures: Ola Nordmann tells, Kari Nordmann listens; neither exists.
- Launch kit, docs only: `docs/launch-post.md` (*Your life in a folder*, the post for Show HN, Lobsters and r/selfhosted: the demo three-liner, the file-is-the-format argument, what the project refuses to do, what is rough, the ask; synthetic throughout), `docs/adapter-bounties.md` (the adapters not yet written — Telegram, Signal, Slack, Teams, Strava, Garmin, Oura, Notion, Mac Screen Time, HomeKit, bank CSVs by country — each with its export format, target profile, tier and a *good first adapter* mark), and four sections in `CONTRIBUTING.md`: the synthetic-data rule, the scrub rule, the clean-room rule for logbook-ts, and one session per worktree. Both pages are in the docs nav.
- `mail` at Takeout scale (`docs/adapters/mail.md`): `logbook add mail` reads a 20 GB `All mail Including Spam and Trash.mbox` in 8 MB chunks with one message in memory at a time, the headers parsed by hand, the MIME tree walked by byte offsets, and no attachment ever decoded or copied — well over 40 MB/s on a laptop, where the standard library's parser managed 13. The body is read from at most 64 KB of its part (`BODY_CAP`); a longer one is cut and flagged `extra.body_truncated` (counted `with the body cut at 64 KB`). Every 10,000 messages a line on stderr gives the messages read, the megabytes and the rate (`run` takes `progress(messages, bytes_read, elapsed)`; `cli._read_progress`). Resumable: the inbox manifest, `inbox/manifest.json` (`logbook/inbox.py`, `Cursor`), keeps per file and per set of options the byte offset of the last message the record holds — the adapter tells the cursor how far each file is read before every draft, `append_many` gains `committed(taken)`, called after every checkpoint, and `add` passes it on — so an interrupted import picks up where it stopped (`resuming at 8,421,113,344 of 21,474,836,480 bytes`) and a finished one reads nothing (`read to the end already`). The cursor holds only while the file is the same (the digest of its first 4 KB and its size); `add --restart` reads from the first byte again, other options are another import, a dry run writes nothing. Threads, dedupe by `Message-ID`, `--only-labels`/`--skip-labels` on `X-Gmail-Labels` and `--since` are as before. Senders and recipients stay `{email, name}` in the line, the candidates for `people merge`; the adapter writes no resolution line. `tests/test_mail_scale.py` generates a 500 MB synthetic mbox (fictional senders at `example.org`; `tests/synthetic_mbox.py`) and asserts the rate and a flat peak RSS under `LOGBOOK_SLOW=1`; its quick tests check every message the standard library's `mailbox` finds is a line here, on 4 KB chunks.
- `logbook demo --years N` (`logbook/labs/demo_life.py`, on the month's `logbook/contrib/demo.py`): the Oslo persona's whole life, from her birth `N` years before the day after 30 June 2026 to that day, deterministic from `--seed`, in phases by age that change the shape of the data — a childhood in Bergen of scanned photos, family entries and stories told about her by Kari and Ola (notes in their voice); school, where the calendar fills and Mia and Jens appear, with a phone from 14 (a thin track, iMessage texts, calls, the first flights declared from memory, the family's cabin weeks as trips); university in Copenhagen (lectures with Hanna and Freja, mail, a term in Barcelona of 131 nights); work (meetings, a monthly transcript, mail three times a week, Zürich twice a year, a month in Lisbon, the flight tracker from 30, Ola back at 29); the move to Oslo at 32 (the office, the cabin weekends, the Copenhagen visits); the watch at 34, so health lines start there; the boat at 35 with a July cruise and day sails on its own AIS track; three weeks in Rome at 37; and the default month at full resolution as the last month of a life of 36 years or more. People drift in and out (Jens last heard in 2005, Mia in 2011, the grandmother Solveig in the photos until 2014, Hanna once more in 2019, Ola out through the university years and back). Old years are thin: forty years are about 115,000 lines, every profile present and the chain verifiable; the month record is unchanged. `--days` and `--years` are one or the other. `event`, `message` and `photo` of the story take a `source` or `provenance`; `demo.generate` takes `years`; `demo.write` writes a story to disk. Docs: `docs/demo.md` (the phases, the knobs, the sizes, what to try). Tests (`tests/test_demo_years.py`): every profile and schema on forty years, trips in 2001 and 2025 and the Barcelona term, flights declared in 2000 and tracked in 2025, health from the watch's first day, the move read from the points, a baby's one-year record, `people` with Jens and Mia a decade gone — read from the channels alone, since `people._together` asks for the night of every day against every stay of the window and takes minutes on forty years.
- `logbook describe keepers [--since DAY] [--limit N] [--photos DIR ...] [--model NAME] [--fetch-model] [--dry-run] [--json]` (`logbook/labs/describe.py`, `docs/describe.md`): a local vision model looks at each keeper's photo (RFC 0024) and writes one factual sentence and a short list of the visible things, as a derived `note/v1` line (RFC 0010) on the photo's day — `source` `description`, tier 2, `raw_id` the photo line's id so a photo is described once however many lanes it is in and however often the command runs, `extra.derived` true, `extra.engine` and `extra.model` naming what wrote it, `extra.photo` the photo as the keeper names it, `extra.keeper`, `extra.things`; the text is the sentence then `Visible: …`, so `search` finds the things. The model sees the pixels and a fixed prompt only — never a name, a date, a location or a face — and is told never to name a person (faces are the library's job, `keepers --people`) and never to guess a place; an answer carrying any word of a face's name the library gave on that photo is refused and counted, never written. The file is found in the record's attachment store (a §1.1 reference under `media`, `attachments`, `file` or `content`) or under `--photos`: an Apple Photos library by the asset UUID under `originals/`, any other folder by file name, case aside, walked once; a keeper whose file is nowhere is a counted skip. The engine is `mlx-vlm` on Apple silicon with `pillow-heif` for HEIC, the new extra `openlogbook[describe]`, default model `mlx-community/Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct-4bit`, temperature 0, the hub offline while it runs; without the engine or the model the command prints the install and fetch lines and exits 2, and `--fetch-model` is the only network use, as `transcribe` and `promises --judge` have it. `show <day>` prints the description under the first keeper row for its photo, indented to the text column; with no keeper standing it is a row of its own. Synthetic fixtures: the Oslo persona's keepers with a faked engine; a test asserts no socket opens during a run.
- `logbook export site <folder> [--tier 1|1,2]` (`logbook/site.py`, `docs/site.md`): the readers as a folder of static HTML — a Year page per year (the page `logbook year --html` writes), every Trip page (`logbook trip --html`, the map inside), a Days index per year (`serve`'s `/days` table, one row per day the record covers, each an anchor), the places (`/places`), the people (an index by days together, and a page per person with the years together and the trips) and a root page — written as plain files with relative links, one inline stylesheet each, no script, no image, nothing fetched, so the folder reads the same from a file manager, under any prefix on any web server, or in twenty years. `serve` written down: `serve.Site` takes `tiers`, `href`, `nav` and `footer` and renders a static page through them, `year.html` and `trip_page.html` take `nav` and `links`, and the rendering is the same either way. Tier-gated exactly like the vault (ADR 0016): the day, days, year and trip readers read the record through `reading.read(..., tiers)`, so a line whose tier is not in `--tier` is in no page, no stay, no night, no companion and no count; the resolution lines (the name overlay) are read whole, as the vault reads them, so a tier-1 site names whoever a tier-1 line confirms present; the ceiling is the `site` entry of `policy/crossing.json` (`policy.site_ceiling`), tier 1 when the file does not name it; tier 3 is refused. Every run appends one `crossing/v1` line (RFC 0011) with destination `site`, the window, the counts, the ceiling and the digest of the folder as written; re-running rewrites only the files whose content changed and deletes nothing.
- The demo persona's forty years, browsable without installing anything: the docs workflow (`.github/workflows/docs.yml`) now runs on every push to `main`, generates `logbook demo --years 40 --seed 1`, exports it with `export site` at tier 1 into `docs/ines/` and publishes it with the docs at `/ines/` on GitHub Pages — the same `gh-pages` branch, the same deploy. Before the build, `scripts/check_synthetic.py` fails the workflow if any page carries an identifier outside CONTRIBUTING.md's synthetic allowlist (an email at a domain other than `example.org`, a phone number outside `07700 900xxx` and `+47 9000 000x`, a nine-digit number in an assigned MMSI range, a URL of any kind) or anything the owner's `pii-patterns` file names, printing `file:line` and a count and never the text: the scrub rule, applied to the rendering.
- `logbook export vault <folder> [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--until YYYY-MM-DD] [--tier 1|1,2]` (`logbook/contrib/vault.py`, `docs/vault.md`): the record as a folder of plain Markdown an Obsidian or Logseq user opens as a vault — one page per day (the Day with YAML front matter: date, weekday, country, the nights either side, places, people, trip), one per named place in `places.json`, one per person confirmed present, one per trip, one per year and a root page, every page linked to the others with wikilinks, so the graph view shows the record's shape: home at the centre, days hanging off their places and people, trips as short chains, a year gathering all of it. Tier-gated exactly like a crossing (ADR 0016): a line is in the vault when its tier is in `--tier` and shows only as a count when it is not (`held back: 1 note`), so the default, tier 1, is the shape of every day with no text of any note, message, transcript or mail, and a person a tier-2 note names is not on the page until tier 2 crosses; the ceiling is the `vault` entry of `policy/crossing.json` (`policy.vault_ceiling`), tier 1 when the file does not name it; tier 3 is refused. Every run appends one `crossing/v1` line (RFC 0011) with destination `vault`, the window, the counts, the ceiling and the digest of the folder as written (one `<sha256>  <path>` line per file, hashed), so the record shows it left. Every page is a function of the record's lines and settings alone, so re-running rewrites only the files whose content changed and deletes nothing. Read a calendar year at a time, as `logbook year` reads it.
- Install is one line on each platform (`docs/install.md`, written for someone who has never opened a terminal, with what each step prints): `brew install bighydro/logbook/logbook` on a Mac, from the new Homebrew formula in `homebrew/` (`homebrew/README.md` says how it is published as the tap `bighydro/homebrew-logbook` and bumped on each release, step 6 of `docs/releasing.md`); `pipx install openlogbook` or `uv tool install openlogbook` everywhere Python is, Windows included, in Terminal. The README's sixty seconds open with one install line per platform. CI builds the formula from source on `macos-latest`, runs its test block and audits it (the new `homebrew` job; the test matrix is unchanged).
- The lab (`docs/labs.md`, `logbook/labs/`): readers still finding their shape, run as `logbook lab <reader>`; read-only,
  no network, not in SPEC §3.2, their JSON free to change. `logbook lab introductions [--json]`: for every person the
  record confirms present at a stay with the owner, the first day it does, where and by which source, and who else
  was confirmed present that day — those known before as the likely introducers, those also new as met together,
  each marked `same_stay` — with the line ids; the blind spots (the record's first weeks, proposals that do not
  count, co-presence as a guess) are printed with the rows and carried under `blind_spots`. `logbook lab chapters
  [--min-trip-nights N] [--min-gap-days N] [--min-home-nights N] [--json]`: the record cut into chapters by
  home-region changes (a home entry in `places.json` may now carry `since` and `until`; without dates the nights
  decide), runs of days with no location line, and trips of 21 nights or more, printed as a table of contents with
  dates, nights, the top places and the top people per chapter. Synthetic fixtures only; nobody in them exists.
- `logbook year YYYY --poster [--sheet A2|A3] [--html PATH]` (`logbook/year_poster.py`, `docs/print.md`): the year on one sheet — twelve columns of thirty-one squares, one a day, each in the ink of where the night was: home, away, aboard, in transit, the four night tokens now in `logbook/print_layout.py`; a day the record has no night for shows the paper; the countries of the year with their days as one footer line; no name of a person, a place or an asset, no photo, no map, nothing fetched. One inline SVG drawn in millimetres of A2 and scaled to the sheet, so A2 and A3 are the same drawing; to `--html PATH`, else stdout.
- `logbook digest --paper [--week YYYY-Www | YYYY-MM-DD] [--html PATH | --json]` (`logbook/week_paper.py`, `docs/print.md`): the week as four pages of A4, set from the Year's typographic constants — the days with their one line each (the line `days` prints), the people confirmed present and the week's keepers, the open promises due in the week, and what was read: the browse lines of the week, titles only, never an address. An ISO week, Monday to Sunday; without `--week` the week of the day given, else of today. Composed from the readers, nothing written. Both render from the demo record as fixtures under `tests/fixtures/demo/`, checked structurally.
- `logbook key init [--recovery] [--recipient age1…]`, `key show`, `key add-recipient`, `key remove-recipient` (`logbook/core/sealing.py`, `logbook/store.py`, RFC 0029 §6): this machine's identity is written to `~/.config/logbook/identities/<owner_id>.txt` (`%APPDATA%\logbook\identities\` on Windows, or where `LOGBOOK_IDENTITY_FILE` or the global `--identity-file PATH` point), readable by its owner only, never in the record and never in a variable on disk; `--recovery` prints a recovery identity once, for paper, and stores it nowhere; `init` refuses to seal with fewer than two recipients; `add-recipient` and `remove-recipient` reseal every sealed line and file to the new list with no hash changed and append one `rekey/v1` line. From then on every tier 2 or 3 line appended is sealed (`Logbook.append`, `append_many`), a tier-1 line never; a writer without the identity refuses tiers 2–3 in one sentence (it cannot dedupe through the blinded index) and writes tier 1, so an unattended `sync ais` needs no key. Needs the new `sealed` extra (`pyrage`); without it the first seal or open names it. `doctor` checks it.
- `logbook seal --all` (RFC 0029 §10.2): seals the tier 2–3 lines written before the record had recipients and the files they name, the ADR 0014 way: new month files under a temporary folder, `prev` and `hash` recomputed from the first changed line, `format` 0.3, a `lineage` entry, the index dropped, one `migration/v1` line with `from_head`, `sealed_lines` and `sealed_attachments`. The plain files are replaced, not kept beside the record, and the command says once that a backup taken before still holds the plaintext.
- `logbook verify` reports sealing: `sealed: N lines, every one opened and checked against its digest`, or `not opened (no identity at …)`, and `plain at tier 2 or 3: N lines; logbook seal --all seals them`; `--keyless` never opens, so a keyed machine can show what a keyless one sees. The first line, `valid — N lines, head …`, is unchanged, so the cross-implementation job still compares it.
- `logbook export FILE` writes sealed lines opened by default (the interchange form a line re-enters by, ADR 0017) and verbatim under `--sealed`; without the identity it refuses and names `--sealed`. `show`, `day` and every reader through the index get opened lines; a reader without the identity lists the day and prints `[sealed note/v1; no identity to open it]` for such a line.
- `export crossing` (RFC 0005, new section *Sealed lines*): `policy/crossing.json` may give a destination a `recipient`; a sealed line crosses with `payload_enc` resealed to it, or under `--open` with `payload_open` = `{payload, salt}` in its place; its hashed fields are the owner's verbatim either way, and the manifest's `sealing` says which. Sealed files are opened, checked and resealed or copied opened. Without a recipient or `--open`, or without the identity, the export refuses.
- The shared trip (RFC 0030, `docs/trips-shared.md`, `logbook/contrib/trip_bundle.py`). `logbook export trip-bundle <trip-id-or-day> --to <member> [--tier 1|1,2|1,2,3] [--attachments] [--out DIR] [--dry-run]` writes a crossing package (RFC 0005) cut to one trip, under the destination's ceiling in `policy/crossing.json` (ADR 0016): the trip's `photo/v1` lines (hashes; the files only with `--attachments`) and standing `flight/v1` lines verbatim in `entries.jsonl`, the derived rows in `trip.json` (`trip-share/v1`: the stays away from home with their nights and labels, the moves, the flights, the people confirmed present with the refs that name them; never the home stay, never the points), and the resolution lines those refs walk through in `resolution.jsonl`. The people, the sender's own refs and the overlay are tier 2, so the default `--tier 1` crosses places and photos and counts the names held back. Every real export appends a `crossing/v1` line naming the trip (`crossing.record`, now shared with `export crossing`). `logbook import trip-bundle <folder> [--dry-run]` checks every digest and every line's hash, refuses a bundle of the record's own, and appends each line as a received line — kind `received`, source `received`, the sender's line verbatim under `payload.line`, `extra.from` the sender's owner id — plus the page as one received line keyed by the bundle id; a `raw_id` the record already holds, as its own or received before, is skipped and counted (`Index.holders`), and the files go into the store. No derived reader takes the kind, so a received photo is nobody's photo and a received resolution names nobody. `logbook trip <id>` gains `shared`: per sender, who was there according to each record (matched through shared refs, else by name; the owner is `you`), the places the records agree on (within 300 m), and a `seen only by` column, in the text, the JSON and the HTML page; `show` lists a received line as `from <sender>: <kind> <source> · …`. Synthetic fixture: Ines's and Ola's records of one cabin weekend (`tests/cabin.py`); nobody in it exists.

### Changed
- `ios-wallet` is replaced by `apple-wallet` (above): the 0.5.0 adapter decoded the IATA BCBP barcode — which carries the passenger's name and the booking reference — to read the flight as `tracked`; the new one reads `pass.json`'s fields and never the barcode, and writes `declared`, since a boarding pass is a plan. `--only wallet`, `--only ios-wallet` and `logbook add wallet` all name the new adapter; lines a record already holds with source `ios-wallet` stand as they are (`flights.EVIDENCE_OF` keeps their rank), and a pass imported again under the new source is a new observation that merges into them. `import-backup` copies the passes below `apple-wallet/` now, not `ios-wallet/`.
- `mail/v1` attachments (RFC 0015 rule 5): an attachment is `{filename, media_type, bytes}` by default, its length counted from the base64 text without decoding it; `sha256` and `path` are present only with `--attachments`, the one time the bytes are decoded. Before, every attachment was decoded and hashed on every import, which is most of the bytes of a mailbox.
- `logbook ledger [--month YYYY-MM | --trip ID] [--json]` (`logbook/ledger.py`): the `transaction/v1` lines (tier 3, never crossing) read back in context. Each transaction is placed at the stay the owner was in at its instant, from the same derivation the Day and the trips read — the place's name at a named place, `aboard <asset>` aboard one, else the coordinates with what is near — with the stay's country by the countries rollup's rule; a transaction the source files by the day only (`date` given, `at` that day's local midnight) is placed by the day's longest stay and says `(by day)`; one the track does not cover is `nowhere`. Each is in its trip when its day is one of the trip's (first day to return day), and the window's trips are listed with their spend; `--trip` takes the id `trips --json` prints and reads only its days. A shared expense's shares are shown per person, each member resolved through the record's resolution lines, else the app's name, the owner as `you`; a face tag is never a resolution. Amounts stay in the line's currency, totals are per currency, nothing is converted; a transaction the source marks deleted is listed, marked and never summed; a correction that `supersedes` a line stands in its place. `rollup money [--year]` sums the same entries per year by month, by country and, when any line has one, by the source's category. `day` gains a `spend` line when the day has a transaction: totals per currency, the count, the merchants (`spend` under `--json`, null when none). Synthetic fixtures: the Oslo persona's fortnight with invented merchants in NOK and EUR. Docs: `docs/ledger.md`.
- `logbook attach import-backup <backup> --only whatsapp,imessage,photos [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--dry-run]`, `logbook attach status` and `logbook attach verify` (`logbook/attach.py`, `docs/attachments.md`): the SPEC §1.1 content-addressed store filled from an iOS backup, counted, and checked. The pass reads, through the index, every standing message and photo line of the sources named (not a retracted one; from `--since` on), finds each file the line names under `extra.media` in the backup's `Manifest.db` by the source's domain and the path below its media folder, and streams it — decrypted on the way when the backup is (`ios_backup.plain_chunks`, over the new `ios_backup_crypto.decrypt_chunks`), the password from `LOGBOOK_BACKUP_PASSWORD` as `import-backup` takes it — into `attachments/<sha256>` through `attachments.write_chunks`, which hashes the bytes on their way into a temporary file and refuses a stream whose digest is not the one the line names (`DigestMismatch`: nothing under the name, one line on stderr naming the file and both digests, exit 1 at the end). A file is never held whole; a digest already present is skipped unread, so a stopped run is resumed by running it again; the same bytes named by several lines or two sources are one file; a file the backup does not hold is counted; a line whose media was missing at import names no digest and is counted `without a digest`. `--only` is required and a disabled source is skipped; `--dry-run` counts and writes nothing. Nothing is appended: the lines already point at the bytes, and until the pass fills them in they are missing attachments, which SPEC §1.1 allows and `status` counts. A progress line every 500 files on stderr. `status` counts, from the index alone, the digests each source's standing lines name (the one attachment a line points at, as `stats` counts), present and missing, and the store's files and bytes, the files no line references and the entries under another name (`--json` too). `verify` streams every file whose name is a digest through SHA-256 and lists the ones whose bytes are not what their name says, exit 1 (SPEC §1.1); another name is counted and ignored. No thumbnails (ADR 0015). Tested on the synthetic backup: four references, three files (two references share one content), a damaged blob refused, a resume, an encrypted backup, a retracted line left alone.
- `apple-photos`: the line carries where the phone keeps the pixels below `Media/` as `extra.media.local_path` (`ZDIRECTORY/ZFILENAME`; a store without the column names no path) and, when the import can reach them, their digest and size under `extra.media` and the digest as RFC 0002 `content_hash`, the same shape `whatsapp` and `imessage` write, so `attach import-backup --only photos` finds the bytes and checks them against the line. `run` takes `media_digest`, a callable that hashes the file at such a path; `import-backup` builds it over the backup itself (`ios_backup.digest`: read from the stored blob, decrypted on the way, never copied), a file not in the backup is `extra.media_missing` and counted; `LOGBOOK_APPLE_PHOTOS_HASH_MEDIA=0` skips it. `logbook add photos <store>` on a bare copy of the library writes the path alone.
- `logbook share day DATE --to NAME [--tier N] [--out FILE]`, `logbook receive FILE [--from NAME]` and `logbook circle [add NAME KEY | key]` (`logbook/share.py`, RFC 0025 at `docs/rfcs/rfc-0025-shared-page-bundle.md`): a shared page, the bundle of ADR 0009 — one local day of the record for one person in the circle, as one zip: the day's lines verbatim (SPEC §2 envelopes, so the receiver recomputes every hash), the attachments they reference under their own digests, the `day-package/v1` of those lines, and `manifest.json` (`shared-page/v1`: `from` owner id, `to`, `date`, `tz`, `created`, `max_tier`, the line count and the count held back, the digests of the two files and of every attachment, `logbook_head`, the signing key) carrying an Ed25519 signature over its RFC 8785 canonical form. The sharing key is made on first use at `~/.config/logbook/share/<owner_id>.key` (mode 0600) and its public half written to `logbook.json` as `share_key`; a record that names a key the machine does not hold refuses to share, naming the file. The tier gate is the destination's ceiling in `policy/crossing.json` (ADR 0016), tier 1 by default, refused above the ceiling naming the file; a line above the tier, a retracted line and a retraction stay home; every share appends one `crossing/v1` line (RFC 0011) with the day's window and the manifest's digest. `receive` verifies the signature under the key `policy/circle.json` holds for the sender (`circle add NAME KEY`, once; never the key the page brought), checks every member name, recomputes every line's hash and checks its tier, day and envelope, checks `package.json` and every attachment against the manifest, and keeps the page under `<root>/circle/<from>/<date>/` with a `received.json` — outside the chain: nothing is appended, `logbook.json` and the store are untouched. The same page again is nothing; a newer one replaces the held one whole; an older one is refused. `logbook day DATE` ends with a `from <name>` section per page held (lines by kind, tier, when shared, the titles of its notes, events, transcripts, mail and calls; `received` under `--json`). The optional extra `openlogbook[share]` (`cryptography`) is imported only when these run; `doctor` lists it. Synthetic fixture: Ines and Ola Nordmann sharing 2026-06-13 both ways (`tests/shared_day.py`), the two bundles and public keys committed under `tests/fixtures/share/` as the cross-implementation fixture; tests include a tampered line refused with or without a re-signature, an over-tier line omitted, and every receiver check.
- A stay aboard an asset is a container (`stays.fold`, `Segment.inside`): the run of the owner's stays and moves aboard one asset is one stay `aboard <asset>`, from the first's start to the last's end, with the run inside it, so the asset's movement never fragments the owner's stay. Boarding takes twenty minutes: the asset's own location lines within the stay radius of the owner's points for `aboard_min_s` (1200 s by default, a new key of `policy/stays.json`) or longer; a boat that passes the quay once no longer boards the owner. Under way the asset's position at an owner's instant is read between its two fixes around it when they are at most twice `aboard_window_s` apart, so an AIS fix every ten minutes still places a passage. The overnight rule counts the stay aboard whole — a passage through the night is a night aboard, never in transit — and the night names the asset and carries the asset's position, the inner stay that held the longest part of the night (`Night.inside`, `Night.position`). `derive stays` prints the container with its run indented and the night with the asset's position (`--json`: `inside` on the stay; `aboard`, `inside` and `position` on every night); `day` builds its `aboard` row from it, shows the position in the header (`night after   aboard REDUCE · 59.3400,10.5500 · away`) and reads the country from it; `trips` makes a night aboard a route element `aboard <asset>` (the asset's name) and counts the trip's nights aboard per asset (`nights_aboard`; the text says `5 nights (2 aboard Solvind)` on a mixed trip); `rollup places` gains an **aboard** section, one stay per run aboard with the run's whole hours (the JSON key `assets` is now `aboard`), while the anchorages inside it remain unnamed places marked `aboard`, and a night aboard counts at the anchorage the asset lay at; `rollup people` and `rollup countries` read the night there too. Synthetic fixture: the yacht REDUCE (MMSI 970123456, which does not exist) motoring 40 km through a night with the persona aboard, and the control case with the persona ashore at a cabin 3 km away ([docs/day.md](docs/day.md), *Aboard an asset*).
- The `encrypted` and `share` extras are one extra, `crypto` (`cryptography` was all either needed): `pip install "openlogbook[crypto]"` reads an encrypted iPhone backup and shares or receives a signed page; `logbook doctor` reports it as `extra:crypto`, and the install line printed when `cryptography` is missing names it. The dev group names the extras it needs (`openlogbook[crypto,ais,mcp]`) instead of repeating their packages.
- The adapter registry is pyproject.toml's `logbook.adapters` entry points alone: every built-in adapter is listed there, in the order `logbook add` tries them on a file, and the package keeps no second list to drift from it; `tests/test_adapter_registry.py` holds the table, the modules under `logbook/adapters/` and the installed metadata to agreement.
- RFC 0025 was two RFCs. The shared page (`docs/rfcs/rfc-0025-shared-page-bundle.md`) keeps the number; the trip bundle is RFC 0030 (`rfcs/0030-trip-bundle-v1.md`). The copies of the RFC index, `location/v1` and `photo/v1` under `docs/rfcs/` are gone and the docs site links to `rfcs/` instead; `activity/v1` and `decision/v1`, never written, left the RFC index; ARCHITECTURE no longer says layer 1 depends on ijson (the Takeout and Dawarich adapters do).
- `tests/test_demo_years.py` (forty synthetic years) is `slow`: it runs on push to main and nightly, not on a pull request. Property tests for the Dawarich adapter, the Takeout `Calendar/` folder, the shared health-export reader and the phone-number normalisation: any input yields only valid lines.
- One package, three tiers: the modules of `logbook` now live under `logbook/core/` (the format and the
  readers a second implementation is held to), `logbook/contrib/` (the adapters, import-backup, sync,
  the exports, the derived readers whose output is a reader's own) and `logbook/labs/` (everything that
  runs a model), with the commands one module per family under `logbook/commands/`. Nothing a user runs
  changes: every command prints the same bytes. Every 0.5 import path (`logbook.store`,
  `logbook.adapters.immich`) still imports the same module, with a DeprecationWarning, until 0.7;
  `docs/migration-0.6.md` lists the moves. `logbook --help` loads no adapter and no model code, and
  `import logbook.cli` is thirteen times cheaper than before.
- `ijson` is the extra `openlogbook[stream]`, not a dependency: the five adapters that stream a large JSON
  export import it when they read one, and `logbook add` names the extra when it is missing. `[share]` is
  an alias of `[encrypted]`: either installs `cryptography`. The `logbook.adapters` entry points list every
  built-in adapter, in the registry's order, and a test keeps the two lists equal.
- `index.sqlite` lives in the user's cache directory, `~/.cache/logbook/<owner_id>/index.sqlite` (`~/Library/Caches/logbook/` on macOS, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\logbook\cache\` on Windows, or `LOGBOOK_CACHE_HOME`), not in the record (RFC 0029 §8): a copy of the folder, by any tool, carries no locator. A sealed line's payload columns come from its opened payload; its `raw_id` is stored as an HMAC under a key kept in the index's `meta` sealed to the recipients, so dedupe and `line_id` match either form and a chat id cannot be read or confirmed from the file; the search table holds no words of a sealed line (`logbook search` does not search sealed lines). An index built without the identity records so, and the first keyed reader rebuilds it. Schema 7. A stray `index.sqlite` left in a record by an earlier version can be deleted.
- `Logbook.attach(data, tier=None)` and `attach_file`: in a record with recipients the file is sealed unless the caller says `tier=1`; `attachment_bytes` and `attachment(sha256)` give readers the plaintext; `backup --verify` checks a sealed file when the identity is here and counts it unchecked when not.
- 22 commands where there were 49, and `logbook --help` on one screen (39 lines, seven groups, one line a command), Decision 1 of `docs/audit-2026-10.md`. Every old top-level name still works, hidden from `--help`: `logbook person Kari` prints `` `logbook person` is now `logbook people` `` on stderr and runs `logbook people Kari` with the same arguments, the same output and the same exit status. The moves: `import-backup`, `inbox`, `attach` and `receive` are `import backup|inbox|attachments|page`; `year`, `trip`, `trips`, `days`, `keepers` and `stats` are `show year|trip|trips|days|keepers|stats`; `person NAME` is `people NAME`; `infer flights|keepers`, `transcribe` and `describe` are `derive flights|keepers|transcripts|descriptions`; `places`, `assets` and `questions` are `setup places|assets|questions`; `ledger` is `rollup ledger`; `tasks` is `promises tasks`; `seal` and `circle` are `key seal|circle`; `share day` is `export share day`; `retract`, `migrate` and `index` are `repair retract|migrate|index`; `sources` is `doctor sources`; `mcp` is `serve mcp`. Nothing any command prints changed. `docs/commands.md` lists the 22 and the old-to-new table, written from `logbook/commands/aliases.py` by `scripts/command_aliases.py`; `logbook <command> --help` now opens with the command's description.

### Spec
- SPEC §3.2 *Readers* now covers every reader the reference ships, not only `show`: for `derive stays`, the standing set of flights and `infer flights`, the with module, `trips`, `day` and `days`, the rollups, `keepers`, `promises` and `sources --gaps`, the inputs, the rules in prose with their constants (the settings of `policy/stays.json` and their defaults; 20 min for a stay and for the shortest inferred leg, 400 m to a home place, 2 km and 3.5 km to an airport, the 22:00–08:00 night, 30 min for a codeshare, 150 km for an inferred flight, 1,000 m and 3,600 s for a calendar entry held at a stay, the confirmed/proposed split of "with"), the shape of each `--json` output, its derived ids, and what is a reader's own (the text, the extractor, a confidence). Two words defined for all of them: *standing* (not retracted, not superseded by a later line of the kind) and *the record's zone* (every reader localises in `logbook.json`'s `timezone`, never a line's `tz`). `show` gains the calendar fold and the hero line, and a flight with no number reads as its route. New §6.1 names the readers the cross-impl job compares on the demo record (`logbook demo --days 30 --seed 1`) and the fields dropped first, with the honest status that no second implementation of a derived reader exists yet. Of logbook-ts's SPEC-QUESTIONS this settles 28, 35, 36, 38, 39 and 41 by rule, and closes 23, 29, 30, 32, 33, 34 and 37 as a reader's own; 24, 25, 27 and 40 were closed by the `show` fixes below. No format bump, no hash-rule change, no code change.
- `logbook add passages <csv> --asset <id> [--routes <dir>] [--dry-run]`: a ship's deck log transcribed as CSV (`dep_local, dep_place, arr_local, arr_place, tz, note, check`; clocks may be missing, the arrival may be empty) → one declared `trip/v1` sea leg per row (RFC 0020, amended: mode `passage`, `subject` the asset, `evidence` `declared`, `name` `Cannes → Genoa Molo Vecchio`, the route as `geometry`) and a `location/v1` line of the asset at each end that can be positioned, so `assets status`, `derive stays`, `day`, `days` and `trips` read the legs as the vessel's own movement (ADR 0018). Positions come from the ECDIS route files in `--routes` (RTZ 1.0; a route matches a leg by the words its name shares with the two places, case, accents and punctuation aside, in either direction), else `places.json`, else a bundled gazetteer of about 250 Mediterranean, US East Coast and Bahamas ports (`logbook/tables/ports.csv`, public positions, nothing invented), else the name stays and the run reports it. An empty arrival is an open leg with a departure line; a `check` value rides under `extra.check`; the dedupe key is the asset, the departure time and the departure place. `add` gains `--asset`, `--routes` and `--dry-run` (the count against the record, nothing written); an adapter's `run` may take `places` (the record's named places) and `report` (lines printed after the counts). `docs/adapters/passages.md`.
- SPEC §5 says what *frozen*, *parked* and *deleted* bind for a payload profile, as RFC 0031 decided; §6.1 says that a frozen
  profile's fixture under `conformance/profiles/` is part of conformance and which parts of it are compared today; §3.1 says
  `migration/v1` is part of the envelope's own section, frozen with it; the *Status* lines of §6 and §6.1 say what the two
  implementations are compared on now. Spec text only: no envelope change, no version bump.
- **Tiers 2 and 3 sealed at rest** (SPEC v0.3 §1, §1.1, §2, §3, §3.1, §4, §6; [RFC 0029](rfcs/0029-encryption-at-rest.md), adopted; [ADR 0020](docs/adr/0020-tiers-2-3-sealed-with-age.md)). A sealed line keeps a plain `payload` that is a reference, `{"schema": "sealed/v1", "of": "<the real schema>", "digest": "<sha256>"}`, and carries the content in `payload_enc`, outside the hash as `id` is: the RFC 8785 text of `{payload, salt}` (16 random bytes), age-sealed (X25519, binary, standard base64) to every recipient `logbook.json` names. The pre-image of §3 is unchanged, so `verify` checks the whole chain without any key, counts the lines it did not open, and, with the identity, opens every sealed line and fails on bytes that do not hash to their digest. `format` is `logbook/0.3`; a `0.2` record is read and written as it is, never migrated, and becomes 0.3 when given recipients. Attachments a tier 2–3 line names are sealed under their plaintext digest (the age header is the marker; raised, never lowered). The format's answer to RFC 0029's three options, the key handling, the migration road and what logbook-ts must implement are in the RFC; §15 says where the implementation parted from the draft. Level 2 conformance: `conformance/sample-logbook-sealed` with the published fixture identity `conformance/identity.txt`, head `c625fd32d553…` in `expected-sealed.json`, verified keyless and keyed in CI; the Level 1 sample stays a `logbook/0.2` record and its head does not change.

### Fixed
- Three Google Takeout adapters skipped every row of a current export as `without a timestamp`: the CSVs Takeout writes today spell the time `2026-10-01 06:59:59 UTC` (a date, a 24-hour clock, the zone's name), which `takeout/times.py` did not read. It reads it now, with or without fractional seconds and with a `+02:00` offset after a space, beside RFC3339 and the two English forms it knew; the spelling is still kept whole in every `raw_id`. `google-takeout-access-log` reads the 2026 header (`Gaia ID`, `Activity Timestamp`, `IP Address`, `Proxiedhost IP Address`, `Is Non-routable IP Address`, `Activity Country`, `Activity Region`, `Activity City`, `User Agent String`, `Product Name`, `Sub-Product Name`, `Activity Type`, `Gmail Access Channel`) and the older one alike: one `event/v1` line per row, the product, sub-product, activity and Gmail channel under `extra`, the country, region and city as the coarse `location` text (`Nesodden, Akershus, NO`) and under `extra`, never a coordinate — and **the IP addresses, the user agent and the account id are never written**: the address and the browser are hashed into the id only, as before, so two accesses in one second from two addresses stay two lines; a record that holds lines from the older reader keeps the same ids. The folder's `Devices - A list of devices (…) used to access.csv` is not the log and is never read. `google-takeout-meet` finds its columns under the 2026 names too (`Start time (UTC)`, an `End time (UTC)` in place of `Duration (seconds)`, `Organiser`), reads a duration as seconds, a clock (`0:30:12`) or words (`1h 2m 3s`), and takes the end less the start when there is no duration; its sniff accepts a history with an end time and no duration. `google-takeout-pay` reads the time under `Time` or `Date`, a bare amount with its code in a `Currency` column, and keys a row without a transaction id by its time, merchant and amount (`pay:<time>:<digest>`, counted `without a transaction id`). Synthetic fixtures for the 2026 shapes under `tests/fixtures/takeout-2026-10/`; `tests/test_takeout_times.py` pins every clock. (Google's Takeout documentation does not list these columns; the 2026 access-log header is from a real export, the Meet and Pay variants are the renamings seen since, and the readers find columns by header words so a further rename reads too.)
- Takeout photos, `imageViews`: a sidecar whose count is a digit character that is not decimal (a superscript, `str.isdigit()` true, `int()` refusing) no longer raises in the reader; `_int` reads decimal digits only and gives `None` otherwise.
- Every reader on a record whose `timezone` (`logbook.json`) this machine's zone database does not know — a name a newer edition of the database has, say — exited with a traceback (`ZoneInfoNotFoundError`) from wherever it first localised a time. SPEC §2 says a reader that does not know the zone says so and never substitutes another: `logbook <command>` now exits 2 with the zone's name and the reason, one place in the CLI entry point for every command. Found by the reader fuzz (`demo --days 2 --seed 5`, timezone set to a zone the host lacks).
- `promises`, who said it: a line of a note, or of a turn a diarizer gave the owner, that begins `Speaker 2:` (or with the name of a participant, of a person the record resolves, or of the owner) belongs to that speaker, never to the owner; the owner is `you` only when the label resolves to one of their identities (`promises.relabel`). Before, every note was the owner's whole, and a mixed turn kept the source's label. A word before a colon that names nobody (`Plan:`) stays text.
- Every reader piped into `head`, or into a consumer that quit already: quiet, status 0. The CLI caught a pipe that broke on a write in the middle of a long listing; a short one — a fortnight of `days`, one `day`, `stats` — sat in the buffer until the interpreter's final flush and reported `Exception ignored in: <_io.TextIOWrapper name='<stdout>'> BrokenPipeError` with status 120. `main` now flushes before it returns, and before a command's own exit status (`sources --gaps` exits 1) is passed on, so the pipe is met there and stdout turned to the null device (`tests/test_broken_pipe.py`, every reader).
- `days` and `day`, the night: a night whose stay centre is within 400 m of a place of kind `home` (`stays.HOME_NEAR_M`, the night rule `trips` already followed) names that place — `Home` — where the row printed the coordinates of a stay at home; an unnamed stay within 5 km of a named place reads `59.9200,10.7400 near Home, 1.0 km`, the route's rule (`trips.coordinates_label`, now shared by `trips` and the Day; the Day printed the bare coordinates); and `days` prints `no location` instead of `in transit` when the day has no location line at all (`night.located` under `--json`), since nothing then says the owner moved — `in transit` is for a day on the road with no stay reaching the minimum.
- `rollup health`, `stats --health` and the Day's health line: HRV is reported in ms, always. `health.ms` reads a line as stored — a value in `s` is multiplied by 1,000, one in `ms` is taken as it is — as `health.bpm` already read a resting rate in count/s; a single 45 ms sample is `hrv 45 ms (1 day)`. `repair health-units` stamps every corrected line with the profile's unit (`repair.HEALTH_UNITS`: `bpm`, `ms`). The Apple Health adapter already wrote HRV SDNN (type 183) in ms unscaled; a test now holds it to that.
- `trips` and `day`, a stay at an airport: labelled by the airport's IATA code and city — `ZRH, Zurich` (`trips.airport_label`; the code alone when the row names no municipality) — where the route printed the row's name and the Day the bare coordinates. An airport with scheduled traffic (the airports table's new `type` column, OurAirports' own: `large_airport`, `medium_airport`; `Airport.scheduled`) holds a stay within 3.5 km of its reference point (`trips.SCHEDULED_AIRPORT_KM`), since the row marks the runway midpoint and a terminal often lies 2–3 km off it, so a stop at a large airport read as coordinates with the city in parentheses; every other row keeps 2 km (`trips.airport_at` is the rule, `Airports.within` the lookup). Every built-in row is `large_airport`, as the generator's filter has always kept; `scripts/make_airports.py` writes the column; an override file may carry it or leave it out. Tested on a synthetic field whose terminal is 2.8 km from the reference point.
- `show`, three places the reference did not read the record as SPEC §3.2 says, found by logbook-ts diffing the conformance sample against it (its SPEC-QUESTIONS 24, 25, 27 and 40): a collapsed run of location points now ends at the last point's `end` when it has one, else at its `at` (it ended at the `at` regardless; one point is still a row of its own and prints its `at`); a payload without a renderer, the `key=value` row, spells a number as RFC 8785 writes it, by value and never by the text the writer stored — `100000000000000000000`, not `1e+20`; `0.000001`, not `1e-06`; `120`, not `120.0` (`chain.number_text`, the serialiser the chain already uses) — while a nested object, list, string, boolean and null keep Python's spelling; and a `message/v1` line whose `chat` is a string, the shape before RFC 0008 that the conformance sample carries, reads as a direct chat of that name instead of raising, and a `chat` of any other non-object shape is read as no chat. Every day of `conformance/sample-logbook` now prints, and 2026-03-01 and 2026-03-06 are pinned whole in `tests/test_show.py` as the cross-implementation fixture; `expected.json` is unchanged, since it carries `seq` and `head` only.
- `day`, `trips`, the pages and `rollup people`, "with" from a note: a name a note says `with` counts only when the record resolves it to a person, the rule transcripts already follow (`present.from_notes`); `with US`, `with XYZ`, a country, an acronym or a name no resolution line knows names nobody, where before it was kept as written. (A real record listed `US` as company.)
- `show` and `day`: one calendar entry several sources carry — two or more `event/v1` lines from different sources (`ics`, `ios-calendar`, `gcal`) with the same start and end, naming the same flight in the title or carrying the same title, case, accents and whitespace aside — is one row, `×N sources` (`logbook/events.py`, `fold`): the same flight in four calendars in two languages reads once, `×4 sources`. `show` folded within five minutes before and printed `ics+ios-calendar`; it now folds on the exact times, as `day` does. Under `--json` the Day's event carries `sources` and every `lines` it stands for; the sources line still counts every calendar's lines.
- `day`: a stop with nothing attached reads `nothing attached` after its place and duration; the kind column already says stop, so the row never read `stop · stop`.
- `trips` and `day`, an unnamed stay: when it is at no large airport (2 km) and near no named place (5 km), the coordinates carry the city of the nearest large airport within 30 km in parentheses — `53.5998,10.0130 (Hamburg)`, the city only, never the airport's name — so a reader sees where a hotel is. The city is the airports table's new `municipality` column (OurAirports' own text, `Oslo (Gardermoen)`; `Airport.city` keeps the part before a parenthesis or a comma; `scripts/make_airports.py` writes it, and the table is regenerated with no other row changed). The airport-name helper #132 left unused is gone with its test.
