- `flight/v1` (RFC 0013 rule 1): a codeshare pair — two lines on one date and route whose departures are within 30 minutes, the operating carrier's number and a marketing carrier's (XY 123 and XY 9123) — is one flight: `reconcile` merges the second into the first under merge-and-supersede, keeping the operating carrier's designator when a producer marked it (`extra.operating` true), else the one first seen; `standing` folds a pair the record already holds unmerged the same way, so `rollup flights` and `trips` show it once (`in XY 561 OSL → ZRH`, not twice). Before, only identical numbers merged.
- `infer flights` never copies a flight number onto a leg that is not the flight the calendar entry names: when a tracked or declared flight in the entry's window already holds the entry's designator on another route, the leg is written with neither `carrier` nor `number`, keyed by date and route (`(date, "", "ZRH>IST")`); `show`, `rollup` and `trips` print such a line as its route alone. Before, a two-day entry naming the tracked outbound put that number on the next day's onward leg.
- `infer flights` emits nothing for a leg between airports under 150 km apart (`MIN_KM`): the points left one airport and reached the other, but it was a drive.
- `infer flights` emits nothing for a leg a tracked flight on the same route already covers under another key — a flight landing after local midnight, a stale point at the origin after departure — counted as `covered` ("legs a tracked flight already covers"). The same key still merges into the tracked flight as before.
- `logbook add` says how many of an export's rows the record already held (same source and `raw_id`) beside what it
  added, where it skipped them silently before: `added 0 lines from dawarich` is followed by `40 already in the record
  (same source and raw_id), nothing written twice` (#79).
- `show` orders a day by the instant of `at`, then `seq` (SPEC §3.2), never by the text of `at`: a line at `10:00:00.5Z`
  sorted before one at `10:00:00Z`. logbook-ts already ordered by the instant (#123).
- `google-takeout-photos`, a sidecar number that is `Infinity`, `-Infinity` or `NaN` (Python's json reads and writes them): a coordinate, altitude, view count or timestamp that is not finite is left out of the line as a value the sidecar never had, never written, so the line canonicalises and appends; `geoDataExif` stands in for a `geoData` with an infinite coordinate, the creation time for an infinite taken time. The adapter already did this (`_finite`, `_stamp`, `_int`); it is now pinned by a test. The property test over albums (`test_any_album_yields_only_valid_lines`) wrote its own sidecar with `allow_nan=False` and crashed (`Out of range float values are not JSON compliant: inf`) before the adapter ran whenever Hypothesis drew an infinite timestamp; it writes them as Python spells them now, and draws non-finite coordinates and timestamps on purpose.
- `verify` on a month file cut inside a line (a crash while writing, SPEC §3) reports the lines before the cut and
  their head, naming the cut line by file and line number in one plain sentence; it reported seq 0 and GENESIS
  and forgot every line before the cut. `logbook verify` prints the seq and head of the lines read on its
  `INVALID` line. SPEC §3 (truncation) and §6 now say so; the envelope and the hashes are unchanged.

### Adapters
- `apple-wallet` (`logbook/adapters/apple_wallet.py`, `docs/adapters/wallet.md`; `logbook add wallet <folder|.pkpass>`, or sniffed, or `import-backup --only wallet`): Apple Wallet passes → `flight/v1` and `event/v1`, from `pass.json` alone. An air boarding pass is one `flight/v1` line, evidence `declared` (RFC 0013, whose producer row changes with it): the airline's word for a seat it sold, not a tracker's record of a flight that flew. Field keys differ per airline, so every value is found by its field's key *or* label (origin/from/dep and destination/to/arr for the route, with the three-letter code the airports table knows in the value, else the label; flight/flightNumber for `XY 561`, `YZ0562` or a bare number with the carrier from an airline field; date/departureDate for the day), or from the pass's semantic tags; the date is the local date of the scheduled departure when the pass names one, else `relevantDate`'s, else a dated field's, and a pass with none is counted (`skipped_no_date`); the seat and the class go under `extra`; `relevantDate` is kept under `extra.relevant_date` and is the line's `at` when no schedule is known, never a scheduled time. A train, boat or bus pass is an `event/v1` line titled `origin → destination` with `extra.transit_type`; an event ticket one with its name, venue and start; a coupon, store card or generic pass one named after the issuer only when it carries a `relevantDate`, else counted. The barcode is never decoded, the back fields, the images, `nfc`, the tokens and the Passes database (`nav.db`, `passes23.sqlite`) are never read, and no field is copied wholesale, so the booking reference and the passenger's name cannot reach a line; the doc lists what is read and what is deliberately not. `raw_id` is `wallet:<16 hex of pass type and serial>@<digest of the mapped line>`: the same pass again appends nothing, a pass the airline updated (same serial, a new `relevantDate` or seat) supersedes the standing line through `flights.reconcile`, and a Flighty export of the flight supersedes both. Three fictional airlines' pass styles, two transit passes, two tickets and the skipped kinds are fixtures under `tests/fixtures/apple-wallet/`.
- `spotify` (`logbook/adapters/spotify.py`, `docs/adapters/listen.md`): Spotify's extended streaming history — the folder `Spotify Extended Streaming History/` of `Streaming_History_Audio_*.json` and `Streaming_History_Video_*.json`, or one of them — → one `listen/v1` line per stream (RFC 0019): `at` when playback started (Spotify's `ts` is the stop; the milliseconds played are taken off it), `end` the stop, `media` `track` or `episode`, the track's name, album artist and album or the episode's name and show as Spotify spells them, `url` the open.spotify.com page of the URI, `played_s`, `service` `spotify`, and under `extra` `ms_played`, `skipped`, `device` (Spotify's `platform`), `reason_start`, `reason_end`, `shuffle`, `offline` and `country`; `raw_id` `spotify:<URI without its prefix>:<ts as spelled>`, so a track streamed twice is two listens and a re-add appends nothing; a stream of 0 ms is a listen with `played_s` 0 and its skip flag; an audiobook chapter (the profile has tracks and episodes) and a row that names nothing are skipped and counted; an older export without `platform` or `skipped` reads with what it has; the IP address and the username are never kept; streamed through `ijson`. `logbook add spotify <folder|file>`, or sniffed.
- `apple-music` (`logbook/adapters/apple_music.py`, `docs/adapters/listen.md`; `logbook add apple-music <file>`, or `add music`, or sniffed): the Apple Music CSVs of Apple's data export (`Apple Music Activity/`), one file at a time by its header, the columns found by name so one the mapping does not need may be missing → `listen/v1`. `Apple Music Play Activity.csv`: one line per `PLAY_END` row — `at` the start, `end` the end, the song (or the older export's `Content Name`), the artist, `duration_s`, `played_s`, and under `extra` `ms_played`, `skipped` (a skip true, a track played to its end false, a pause absent), `device`, `end_reason`, `offline`; `raw_id` from a digest of the artist and title with the start as spelled, the file carrying no track id; a start, a lyric view and the other events counted, not lines. `Apple Music Library Tracks.csv`: a snapshot per last-played time as `apple-podcasts` writes — `raw_id` `apple-music:<track identifier>@<last played>`, title, artist, album, `duration_s`, `extra.play_count`, `extra.skip_count`; a track never played counted. `Apple Music - Play History Daily Tracks.csv`: coarsely, one line per track per day at the first hour listed in the record's zone, the day's `played_s`, `extra.hours`, the counts. The three files carry three keys and do not dedupe against one another, said in the docs. The Apple ID and the IP address are never kept.
- `screentime` (`logbook/adapters/screentime.py`, `docs/adapters/screentime.md`): `logbook add screentime [--mac DB | --backup DIR] [--dry-run]` → one `app-use` line per stretch an app was in use — kind `app-use`, tier 2, an `event/v1` payload with the app's name as `title` when known (else its bundle id), the calendar `Screen Time`, and under `extra` the `bundle_id`, `app`, `device`, `device_name`, `duration_s` and how it was `observed`. `--mac [DB]` reads a Mac's `knowledgeC.db` (this Mac's own without a path, behind Full Disk Access, which a store that will not open names): the `/app/usage` stream's sessions, each with the device of its source row (`mac` for this Mac's own); the other streams are never queried, so no window title, URL or document name reaches a line, and a test holds the adapter to it. `--backup DIR` copies Screen Time's own store, `RMAdminStore-Local.sqlite`, out of an iOS backup into `inbox/ios-backup-<udid>/screentime/` as `import-backup` does — `import-backup --only screentime` reads it too, a new source — and reads its hourly blocks: one line per app per hour laid from the top of the hour for its seconds (`observed` `hourly_total`, `apple_category` Screen Time's own), the phone named through `ZCOREDEVICE` and its apps through `ZINSTALLEDAPP` when the store has them; Safari's per-domain rows are skipped and counted with the domain never read. `raw_id` `<device>:<bundle id>:<start>`, so a re-import appends nothing and a session a store holds twice is one line. Every table's columns are confirmed with `PRAGMA table_info`; a table or column needed that is missing is one error naming the store. `show` prints a line as `Safari · 25 min · mac`. Synthetic fixtures with the real table and column names.
- `apple-photos` (`docs/adapters/photos.md`, new): the names the owner gave the faces in the People album (`ZPERSON.ZFULLNAME`, else `ZDISPLAYNAME`; a merged person under its merge target's) ride along under `extra.faces`, sorted, beside the ids under `people`, which stay ids as RFC 0002 says — the adapter's extension inside the payload, a proposal of presence a reader never confirms. The favourites and the Art album become `keeper/v1` lines (RFC 0024) in the same `add photos` or `import-backup` run, right after the photo lines: the photo line's id is looked up by `(source, raw_id)` through the index, the keeper is `keepers.draft` of that line and lane, and a `(keeper-inference, <line id>:<lane>)` the record already holds, retracted or not, is skipped, so a re-import and `infer keepers` after it write nothing twice; the run says `keepers: N new from M marked photos of P (K already in the record)`, and `--dry-run` counts them the same way and writes nothing (a module's `KEEPERS` flag tells `add` so; every other photo library keeps `infer keepers`). The synthetic `Photos.sqlite` the tests build carries the Oslo persona's faces.
- `weather` (`logbook/adapters/weather.py`, RFC 0026 `weather/v1`, `docs/adapters/weather.md`): `logbook sync weather [--since DAY] [--until DAY] [--dry-run]` writes one tier-1 line per local day and place the day was spent at — the overnight stay and every stay of three hours or more, each rounded to a tenth of a degree (about 10 km) and deduped per day, so no exact place leaves the machine — with the day's lowest and highest temperature, precipitation, strongest wind, WMO weather code, sunrise and sunset from Open-Meteo's free archive API (no key; the forecast API for the last seven days), `evidence` `external`. One request per place and run of days (split only at a gap over a week), a second's pause between requests, every day of a response cached under `inbox/weather/<lat>_<lon>/<day>.json` so a re-run never refetches; a failed request is said, the rest still go out, the run exits 1 and the watermark (`state/weather.json`, the last day covered, a week's lookback) waits. Explicit command only: `sync --all` and the schedule leave it out unless `LOGBOOK_WEATHER=1` is set (`~/.config/logbook/sync.env`), no reader opens the network, and `policy/import.json` disables it either way. The clustering and the readers' side live in `logbook/weather.py`; the tests run the sync against a fake Open-Meteo and check every request URL for a coordinate with more than one decimal.
- Six more Google Takeout products, one sub-adapter each in `logbook/adapters/takeout/`, every one answering to `takeout-<name>` as well as `google-takeout-<name>` (`adapters.TAKEOUT`, `ALIASES`), with synthetic fixtures in Takeout's own layouts under `tests/fixtures/takeout/` and the owner's page `docs/adapters/takeout.md`: `pay` (`Google Pay/Google transactions/*.csv` → one `transaction/v1` line per payment, RFC 0021, tier 3 and never lowered, provider `google-pay`, the amount signed from the owner's side — the sign in the text, else negative unless the status, product or description says refund, received, reversal or credit — and the currency from the code or symbol in the amount text, a `kr` row skipped and counted; `Google Pay/Passes/*.json` → an event ticket, a transit pass or a flight pass as one `event/v1` line, tier 1, with venue, issuer, seat, gate and terminal and never the holder's name, loyalty cards, offers and gift cards counted); `chat` (`Google Chat/Groups/<conversation>/messages.json` → one `message/v1` line per message, tier 2, the chat direct or group from the folder and `group_info.json`, `from_me` from `owner_emails` or the one user under `Users/`, the sender as an email ref with the name shown, the first attached file hashed beside the message and stored only with `--attachments`, reactions and links under `extra`; `raw_id` `chat:<message_id>`); `meet` (`Google Meet/Call history/*.csv` → one `call/v1` line per call, tier 1, outgoing when the organizer is the owner, the organizer as the counterparty otherwise, `service` `meet`, the meeting code and participant count under `extra`); `contacts` (`Contacts/All Contacts/*.vcf` → one entity per card, a person or a company, and one `resolution/v1` line per email and phone normalised as `ios-contacts` normalises them, **merged** with the people the record already resolves: a card whose ref the record knows takes that entity's id and writes no line for the ref, so a person resolved from the phone's address book is never minted twice; a label folder repeats nothing); `access-log` (`Access Log Activity/Activities - *.csv` → one `event/v1` line per access to a Google service at **tier 3**, title `<product>: <activity>`, the city and country as `location`, the address, user agent and device under `extra`); `activity` (`My Activity/<Product>/MyActivity.json` → a search or a visited result as a `browse/v1` visit with `google-<product>` as the browser, an app opened under Android as a visit to its Play Store page, a YouTube entry through the YouTube adapter's own mapping so the two sources dedupe, a Chrome entry skipped for the Chrome adapter, ads and other activities skipped and counted). Each CSV is read by what its headers say, not by position; `takeout/times.py` reads the three clocks Takeout writes to one UTC instant. The YouTube adapter no longer claims another product's `MyActivity.json`.
- `policy/import.json` has a default list (`policy.DEFAULT_IMPORT`): `google-takeout-access-log` and `google-takeout-activity` disabled with a reason (every sign-in to every Google service; every search and app opened; opt in), everything else enabled. `init` writes it, and so does the first read of a record without the file; the owner removes an entry to opt in.
- `logbook add --dry-run`: the adapter runs, the drafts are counted against the record by `(source, raw_id)` through the index — `<name>: N lines would be added, M already in the record (dry run, nothing written)` — with what the adapter skipped and why, and nothing is written: not a line, not an attachment (the store callbacks are no-ops), not a note for a sentence.
- An adapter whose `run` takes `resolved` gets `{(kind, value): entity id}` for every ref the record resolves, from the resolution lines standing through their aliases (RFC 0006), so an import of people can merge with the record instead of minting again (`cli._resolved`; `google-takeout-contacts` is the first).
- `google-takeout-fit` (`takeout-fit`), the `Fit/` folder of a Google Takeout → `health-sample/v1` (RFC 0014) at tier 3,
  with the types and units `apple-health` writes, every line naming the device and the app Fit recorded it from
  ([page](docs/adapters/takeout-fit.md)). `All Data/`: steps and distance summed into quarter-hour buckets per device,
  heart rate at its own resolution capped at one reading a minute, weight, body fat, and sleep stages as spans (light as
  `core`); the raw streams of a type Google also merged are counted, not read twice; calories, activity segments and the
  other streams the profile has no type for are counted, never mapped to a near type. `All Sessions/`: one `workout` span
  per session with its aggregates under `extra`, a sleep session as the night in bed. `Daily activity metrics/`: a day's
  quarter-hour file as buckets, and a day-long `steps` and `distance` span for a day no stream touched, the day's
  calories, Heart Points, Move Minutes and heart-rate figures under `extra`; the coordinate columns never read. The
  `Fit/` folder, each subfolder and any one file are sniffed; `Activities/` (TCX) is not read. A quarter hour Apple
  Health also counted stays a second line under its own source: nothing dedupes across sources yet. Synthetic fixture.

### Readers
- `logbook rollup listen [--year YYYY | --since YYYY-MM-DD --until YYYY-MM-DD] [--json]` (`logbook/listen_rollup.py`, `docs/rollups.md`, `docs/adapters/listen.md`): the `listen/v1` lines standing (RFC 0019; a correction that `supersedes` a line wins, the latest of several; a retracted line is out) summed up per calendar year — listens, hours played (`played_s` summed; a line with no playhead, a Shazam tag, is a listen with no hours and counted under `untimed`), skipped lines (`extra.skipped`), lines per service, the podcast episodes with their hours, the top ten artists by hours then listens (the tracks' performers as the services spell them, never resolved), and every month the window touches with its hours, listens and skips, a month with no listen an em dash and never a zero. The window is clipped to the days the listen lines cover and read through the index alone, with no reading of the track; nothing is written; under `--json` every number carries the ids of its lines. In its own module beside `rollup.py`, in its manner, with `rollup.py` untouched; `rollup` lists `listen` beside the other kinds.
- `logbook rollup attention [--year YYYY | --since YYYY-MM-DD --until YYYY-MM-DD] [--by month|week] [--json]` (`rollup.attention`, `docs/rollups.md`): the `app-use` lines standing (a retracted line out, a superseded one out) summed per calendar year, or per month or ISO week with `--by`, as hours by app and by five categories — communication, browser, media, work, other. An app is its bundle id (the Mac's Safari and the phone's are two rows); a small built-in table (`logbook/apps.py`) names and sorts the common apps under both their Mac and iOS bundle ids, a word in an unknown id (`mail`, `chat`, `music`, `code`) sorts it, and the owner's `policy/apps.json` — `{"<bundle id>": "<category>" | {"name", "category"}}` — wins over both, read at rollup time and never written into a line, so a renamed app re-sorts a year without a new line; a category outside the five stops the rollup naming the file. Every period the window touches is a row: one with no line is an em dash (`by_category` null under `--json`), one with lines carries every category, a category with no app at zero. Under `--json` each app carries its `bundle_id`, `app`, `category`, `hours`, `seconds`, `sessions`, `devices` (seconds per device) and `lines`. Read through the index alone, clipped to the days with an app-use line; nothing is written.
- `logbook keepers --people [--since DAY] [--until DAY] [--lane LANE] [--json]` (`keepers.people_by_month`, `docs/adapters/photos.md`): who appears on the keepers standing, per local month — the faces the library named on each keeper's photo line (`extra.faces`) and the people it tagged by id (`people`), resolved through the resolution lines where a name is exactly a label and listed as written otherwise (`no person`) — with how many keepers each is on, by lane, and the keeper lines under `--json`. Every row is `proposed`: a face never confirms, and the view writes nothing. The keepers' photo lines are joined by id through the index (`Index.by_ids`, new: the few lines a reader joins by id).
- `present` (the with module), the Day, the pages and `trips`: the names a photo library gave its faces, `extra.faces` on a `photo/v1` line, are **proposed** company at the stay that holds the photo, confidence 0.5, never confirmed — a note, a transcript or a timed calendar entry confirms, and a face then rides along as a further reason. A name is resolved to a person only when it is exactly the label a resolution line carries, case and spacing aside (`present.by_label`): `Kari` alone is not `Kari Nordmann`, since nobody wrote the name at the stay (the loose first-name rule of a note does not apply); a name the record does not know is listed as written with no person; a face the line names both by id and by name is one entry; the owner's own face is never their company. `rollup people`, `people` and the days together still count the confirmed set only.
- `logbook search TEXT [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--until YYYY-MM-DD] [--kinds note,transcript,message,mail,event] [--tier 1|2|3] [--limit N] [--json]` (`logbook/search.py`, `docs/search.md`): full-text search over the record through one FTS5 table of `index.sqlite` (`search`; schema 6, so an older index is rebuilt once by the first reader). The table holds the words of every line of a kind that carries any — `note`, `transcript`, `message`, `mail`, `event`, `task`, `highlight`, `voice-memo`, `browse`, `watch`, `listen`, `trip`: every string of the payload at any depth but the ids, digests, stamps and types, and a transcript's text read from the attachment store as the promises reader reads it, one `Speaker: text` per turn — never a location point, a photo or a health sample. It is written beside the line's row by `Logbook.append` and `append_many`, in the same transaction, so a line appended a second ago is found with no rebuild, and rebuilt with the rest by `logbook index`. The query is literal: a word matches itself, case and accents aside (`zurich` finds `Zürich`) and never a stem (`run` does not find `running`, in English or in German; the tokeniser is `unicode61` with no stemmer); a run in double quotes is a phrase in that order; a term ending in `*` matches by prefix; every term must match; FTS5's operators (`AND`, `OR`, `NOT`, `NEAR`, a colon, a caret) are words, never syntax, so no query is a syntax error. Ranked by FTS5's bm25 and cut to `--limit` (50) inside the query — the kinds, the tier and the window are columns of the table, the join to `lines` one lookup per hit kept, so a name in two million lines answers in well under a second (`tests/test_search.py`, `LOGBOOK_SLOW=1`) — then shown grouped by local day, the latest day first, the lines of a day in time order: the row `show` prints for the line (its local time, kind, source and the profile's summary, with the names the record resolves), under it a snippet of up to sixteen words around the match with each matching word in `[brackets]`, and a count. Tiers 1 and 2 by default; tier 3 — money, health, the transcripts at 3 — only with `--tier 3`, as `export crossing` and `mcp` have it, and a search that finds nothing says when tier 3 was left out. A retracted line is never a hit. `--json` is the query (with the FTS5 expression it became), the hits by day with `rank`, `snippet` and `summary`, and `more`. Nothing is written. A SQLite built without FTS5 builds an index with no search table, and the command says so; every other reader is unaffected. The `show` row's text part is `cli._line_text` now, shared by both.
- `logbook trip <id-or-date> [--html PATH] [--json]` (`logbook/trip_page.py`, `docs/year.md`, *One trip*): one of `trips`' runs of nights away read back, named by its id (`trip:2026-06-15:2026-06-20`, which `trips` now prints as the last part of every row) or by any day inside it, the return day included. Composed from the readers over one reading of the days around the trip (a week either side, widened while the run touches the window's edge, so a week is read from a month): the **route** as the stays slept at, in night order, each with its nights — the anchorages of a week aboard, one stay each with the asset beside them, where `trips` folds the week into `aboard <asset>`; consecutive nights within 200 m one stay; a night in transit a stay with no place — and the **legs** between them in great-circle kilometres; the **days** from the leaving day to the return day, one line each as `days` prints them; the **flights** in and out; the **people** confirmed and, apart, the ones only proposed (a person confirmed at one stay is never a proposal at another: `trips.companions`, which `trips` now uses for its own list); the **nights aboard** per asset; the **keepers** per day by lane with the photos' names; the **health** of the span as one period (`rollup.health_summary`, the rollup's own rule lifted out); and the **spend**, every `transaction/v1` line dated inside the span summed per currency with a minus sign for money that left, when the record holds any. `--html` writes one self-contained page — inline CSS, no script, no asset, everything escaped, printable — whose route section opens with an inline SVG map drawn from the stays' coordinates: an equirectangular projection about the route's mean latitude, one path per leg (dashed across a night in transit), one mark per point with the numbers of the stays at it, a scale bar; no tiles, nothing fetched. A day at home, an id that names no trip and a record without a home place each say so. Nothing is written. Tested on the demo record's yacht week and the persona's Zürich trip, the SVG's one path per leg included.
- `day` shows one `weather` row after the health line when the day has `weather/v1` lines — the place of the night when the day has one, else the first, `· 2 places` when it was spent in more than one; `--json` carries the row with every place under `places` — and `serve` a Weather heading; `year` adds a `weather` row and an HTML section with the span's summary (`weather.span`: the coldest and warmest day, the precipitation summed over the days by the wettest place of each, the wet days at 1 mm or more, the strongest wind, how many of the days have a line); `trips` says the same for each trip's days, first to return day (`weather` in the JSON). A record without weather lines shows nothing new.
- `logbook promises --judge [--limit N] [--model NAME] [--fetch-model]` (`logbook/judge.py`, `docs/promises.md`): a local instruct model reads each candidate the rules found — the sentence marked among the two sentences either side of it in the same transcript or note, across turns, each with who said it; the day, the title, the owner's name and the names the record resolves in that line — and answers one JSON verdict at temperature 0: `is_commitment`, `by` (`owner`, a name, `unknown`), `to`, `what` (the speaker's words shortened), `due` (`YYYY-MM-DD` or null) and `confidence` (0 to 1). The engine is `mlx-lm` on Apple silicon, the new optional extra `openlogbook[judge]`, with `mlx-community/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-4bit` by default and `--model` for any MLX instruct model; it is imported only when `--judge` runs, the hub is offline while it runs (no socket is opened, as `transcribe` has it), a model not on the machine stops the command naming `--fetch-model`, and without the extra `--judge` stops with the message that names it. Never a cloud model. The verdicts are kept in `policy/promises-cache.json` by candidate id with the engine and model that gave them, rewritten after each one, so an interrupted run keeps what it did and a candidate is never judged twice; an answer with no whole verdict is counted and tried again next run. `logbook promises` now shows the judged commitments at confidence 0.6 or above (`promises.THRESHOLD`) and says how many candidates are unjudged; `--all` shows every candidate; a judged row carries the confidence, `to <name>` and, for a non-commitment, `no`; under `--json` every proposal carries its `judgement` or null, and the report `judged_only`, `threshold`, `judge` and `unjudged`. `promises done <id>` is unchanged and the only write to the chain. Each proposal carries the context the judge sees (`Proposal.before`, `.after`, `.names`). Tested through a fake engine: the prompt's attribution and context window, the cache, the threshold, the limit, the no-socket guard and that nothing reaches the chain but through `done`.
- `logbook year YYYY [--html PATH] [--json]` (`logbook/year.py`, `docs/year.md`): one calendar year read back, composed from the readers that exist over one reading of the year's days (the days the owner's track covers, clipped as `rollup --year` clips): the days per country (`rollup countries`), the nights home, away, in transit and aboard each asset (`rollup nights`), the trips in order with their nights, route, flights in and out and companions (`trips`), the flights with their kilometres and evidence (`rollup flights`), the places by nights — named, assets, the top unnamed clusters — with the people confirmed there (`rollup places`), the people by days together (`rollup people`), the health lines by month (`rollup health --by month`), the keepers by month and lane (RFC 0024), and twelve picks, one day each: for every month the day with the most evidence — the attachments across the Day's rows plus the people confirmed plus one for a flight or a new place (a named place not stayed at earlier in the year, or an unnamed stay more than 300 m from every earlier one; home never) — of equals the day with more lines, then the earlier; a month with no line is `no days`. Each pick is read with the day reader (`day.read`, the night before in it) and printed as `logbook day` prints it; under `--json` the Days are inside and every day's evidence is under `scored`. `--html` writes one self-contained page — inline CSS, no script, no asset, nothing fetched, everything escaped — that prints with table headers on every sheet and no pick split; `--json` is one object with the chain head it was read at. Nothing is written. Tested on the 365-day demo record and the persona's fortnight (the month-pick rule, hand-checked).
- `places propose` reads the index, not the files (`reading.owner_track`): the owner's location points and the registered assets', the evidence that promotes a stay and the retracted ids are columns of `index.sqlite` now (`subject`, `lat`, `lon`, `end`; schema 5, so an older index is rebuilt once on the first reader), `--since`/`--until` are cut in the query, and the clustering runs from the rows — no month file is opened, however long the window, and the cost is linear in the points of the window (half a million lines over two years in four seconds, 2.8 million points in the window in under half a minute, on a small cloud box) where before every line of the window was parsed from the files. The only lines read whole are the Google Timeline visits, found through the index by their `raw_id`. Same output as before, pinned on the demo record (`tests/fixtures/demo/places_propose.*`); a generated two-year record of half a million lines asserts not one file is opened (`LOGBOOK_SLOW=1`). Along the way `derive stays` attaches evidence and matches an asset's track by binary search instead of a pass over the window per cluster, so a long window no longer grows quadratically; a stay aboard an asset is unchanged. The points `propose` clusters are the owner's and those of the assets `assets.json` names: a subject the registry does not name is not read, since nothing of the owner's can be aboard it.
- `logbook days [--from YYYY-MM-DD] [--to YYYY-MM-DD] [--json]` (`logbook/days.py`, `docs/day.md`): a window of the record read back one line per day, composed from the Day of each (`day.of_reading`, the Day built from a reading the caller holds; `day.health_rows`, `day.counts`, `day.health_text` and `day.distance_text` exposed for it, the Day's own output unchanged). Each line: the date and weekday; where the night was spent (the night after: a named place, `aboard <asset>` or the coordinates with the nearest large airport's city, and the country when the night is away; `in transit`); the kilometres moved (every move that started on the day, the flights' included; aboard an asset, its passages); the flights (`XY 561 OSL→ZRH`); the stays (a stay or a run aboard an asset) with how many lines are attached across the day's rows; the people confirmed present; the health triple when the day has one; and a gap marker naming every usual source with no standing line that day — usual: with a line on at least four in five of the window's logged days, counted on the index in one aggregate (`Index.source_days`, new), so a tracker that fell silent is seen and a calendar that spoke once is not missed daily. The window is read in chunks of a month through one `reading.read` each (the chunk's days and the day before; the chunk's health lines in one indexed query), so a year is a dozen readings, never one per day, and the lines stream out as each chunk is read: a year of the demo record prints in seconds, the first line within one. Both bounds default to the days the owner's track covers. `--json` is JSON Lines, one object per day. Nothing is written.
- `logbook rollup health [--year YYYY | --since YYYY-MM-DD --until YYYY-MM-DD] [--by month|week] [--json]` (`logbook/rollup.py`, `docs/rollups.md`): the `health-sample/v1` lines (RFC 0014) summed up per calendar month, or per ISO week (`2026-W23`) with `--by week` — sleep (the mean of the nights' hours and how many nights had a line; a night the asleep stages of the night that ends on the day, per device the union of their spans, the longest device, rules 4 and 5), steps (the mean of the days' counts and how many days; a day the larger device per quarter hour, summed), resting heart rate (the mean, lowest and highest of the days' rates in bpm, and how many days) and HRV when the record has any (the mean of the days' SDNN in ms, and how many days). A correction that `supersedes` a line wins, the latest when a line was corrected more than once; a retracted line is out. Units as stored: a resting rate in `count/s` is read in bpm (×60), one in `bpm` or `count/min` as it is. Every month or week the window touches is a row, and a field no day of the period has a line for prints an em dash and is `null` under `--json`, never a zero. The window is clipped to the first and last day with a health line and read through the index alone, with no reading of the track. Under `--json` every number carries the ids of the lines it came from. `--by` is refused for any other kind. `docs/rollups.md` is new: every rollup in one page.
- `logbook/health.py` (`stats --health`, the Day's health line, `rollup health`): the day rows carry `hrv`, the mean of the day's heart-rate variability readings in whole ms (`stats --health --json` and the Day's JSON gain the field; the tables are unchanged); a resting reading is read in the unit its line states (`count/s` ×60 to bpm), where before the value was taken as bpm whatever the unit; of several lines that supersede the same line the latest stands and the earlier corrections are out with it, where before every correction stood.
- `logbook rollup places [--year YYYY] [--with]` (`docs/rollups.md`): every named place and asset now carries its **nights** — the days whose overnight stay is there — beside its stays, hours, first and last visit and company; and each year lists the top five **unnamed** clusters, the owner's stays at no named place grouped as `places propose` groups them (300 m) and ranked by hours, each under the stay id `places name` takes, with its label (the coordinates; `aboard <asset>`; `near <place>, x km` within 5 km; else the city of the nearest large airport, the `trips` rule), its nearest named place, nights, stays, hours and company. Each person at a place carries their `stays`, `days` and `nights` there; a night counts for a person when the evidence that put them at the stay is dated the night's day, so dinner on the 16th is that night together, not every night of the stay. `--with` is the place × person table — one row per person per place, stays, days and nights — printed as a table and, under `--json`, as `with` per year; it goes with `places` only.
- `logbook rollup people [--year YYYY]`: the confirmed set of the with module only. A tagged face and an all-day entry's attendee are proposals and no longer a listed person, a counted line or a `proposed` number: a person the record knows only by a face is not in the rollup (the Day and the pages still propose them); the owner is never listed. Each person gains `nights` together (the `--with` rule) and `stays` shared; an unnamed shared place reads with its city or its nearest named place instead of bare coordinates. `unresolved` keeps the confirmed names the record resolves to no person.
- `present.Evidence`: the window's evidence lines (events, transcripts, notes, photos) bucketed by every local day their span touches, so the company of a stay is one lookup over the lines of its days and never a pass over the whole window per stay or per place; `rollup places` and `rollup people` read through it, from one reading of the window through the index and one derivation of the stays.
- `logbook day YYYY-MM-DD [--json]` (`logbook/day.py`, `docs/day.md`): one calendar day read back. The header — the date, the night before and the night after (a named place, `aboard <asset>` or the coordinates; home, away or in transit), the country of the day from the night's stay by the `rollup countries` rule (else the longest stay of the day), the day's all-day entries. The timeline — the owner's stays, stops and moves from `derive stays` that touch the day, clipped to it for display and keeping their real span, and the flights of the day from the `flight/v1` lines standing (`flights.standing`, the merged set) as rows of their own with their evidence (`tracked`, `inferred`, `declared`); a run of segments aboard one asset is one stay `aboard <asset>` with the berth, the passage and the anchorage inside it, so the asset's movement never fragments the stay; a move with no points that lasts a silence or more and is not a flight is a `gap`, said so, and places nothing. Under each row its attachments, named for events, transcripts, notes, mail threads, calls and keepers and counted for messages and photos, and `with`, the people there split into confirmed (a note's declaration, a transcript's speaker, a timed calendar entry's attendee) and proposed (a face, an all-day entry's attendee), never the owner; a move carries attachments but no company. Then the lines inside no row (`unplaced`: the calendar's plan where the track has nothing), the health line (sleep hours, steps, resting heart rate) and the sources with their newest line's time, so a tracker's silence is seen. Read through the index from one `reading.read` of the day and the day before; nothing is written, not even `policy/stays.json`. Under `--json` every row carries the ids of its lines.
- `present` (the with module): an all-day calendar entry's attendees are proposed (confidence 0.3), never confirmed — the entry names no hour; and only when the entry is located at the stay (its coordinates or a named place within 1 km), since an all-day entry with no location overlaps every stay of its day and places nobody at any one of them; `present` and `company` take `owner` (`present.Owner`, built by `owner_of` from `owner_id` and `owner_emails` in `logbook.json`, `policy/owner.json` and the resolution lines) and never list the owner as company.
- `logbook/health.py`: the `stats --health` summary (sleep, steps, resting heart rate per day, RFC 0014 rules 4 and 5) moved out of the CLI so the Day shares it; a health line another health line `supersedes` (a `repair health-units` correction) is out and the correction stands, as a retracted line already was; each row carries the ids of the lines its numbers came from (`stats` drops them).

### Commands
- `logbook add <folder>` walks into subfolders — four levels down and no further — so a whole Takeout root reaches `My Activity/<product>/MyActivity.json` and `YouTube and YouTube Music/history/*.json`, which a one-level walk never saw. A folder an adapter reads as a whole (`Google Pay/`, `Keep/`, the root that holds `Google Photos/`) is handed to it and the subfolders that adapter also claims are left to it, so nothing is read twice; every other subfolder is walked the same way. A file no adapter reads is counted, not named, unless `--verbose` (new) is passed; a file given on the command line is still named and still exits 2. The folder's last line names each folder something was read from, relative to the one given: `Takeout: read 3 folders — Google Meet, Google Pay, YouTube and YouTube Music/history; 12 files with no adapter (--verbose names them)`. `cli.FOLDER_DEPTH` is the bound; hidden files and folders are never exports.
- `logbook backup DEST [--keep N] [--verify]`, `logbook backup list DEST` and `logbook backup restore SNAPSHOT TARGET` (`logbook/backup.py`, `docs/backup.md`): the record copied to another disk and verified there. A snapshot is `DEST/<owner_id>/<timestamp>/` (UTC, `2026-06-02T071500Z`, sortable, no colon), the record's files at their own paths — `logbook.json`, every file under `logbook/`, `policy/`, `places.json`, `assets.json`, `attachments/`, `notes/` — and never `index.sqlite`, `inbox/` or `state/`. A file whose size and modification time are those of the same path in the previous snapshot is a hard link to it, as `rsync --link-dest` does it, so every snapshot after the first costs the bytes that changed and is still a whole record on its own; a filesystem that refuses the link gets a copy. The copy is pure Python and streams a chunk at a time. It is written under `.<timestamp>.partial`, `verify` runs on it by the files alone, its head must be the head `logbook.json` named when the copy began, and only then is it renamed into place: a copy that does not verify, or a record a line was appended to while it was copied, is removed and the command exits 1 saying so; `--verify` also hashes every attachment in the copy against its name (SPEC §1.1). `--keep N` prunes the oldest snapshots of this owner to N after the new one verified. A destination inside the record, or under a folder a sync client owns (`doctor.cloud_folder`: iCloud Drive, Dropbox, OneDrive, Google Drive), is refused before anything is written, exit 2. `backup list DEST` shows each owner's snapshots oldest first with the lines and head their `logbook.json` claims, the size, and the bytes not shared by a hard link with an earlier one; it needs no record. `backup restore SNAPSHOT TARGET` copies — never links — into a folder that does not exist or is empty, never over anything, verifies the result and prints its head; the next reader builds the index. `docs/backup.md` carries a launchd agent and a systemd user timer for a nightly run beside `sync --install-schedule`'s.
- `logbook tasks [--open] [--propose-done] [--json]` and `logbook tasks done <id> [--evidence LINE-ID]` (`logbook/taskdone.py`, `docs/tasks.md`): the record's tasks read back, every `task/v1` line (RFC 0016) as the task it describes — per source id (the `raw_id` before its last `@`) the standing snapshot with the newest suffix is the task's state, a retracted line out — with its day, status, due day, list and a sixteen-hex id that is a digest of the source id, the same for every snapshot. `--propose-done` asks, for each open task, whether the record says it was done: within the fourteen days after the task's `at`, a `mail/v1` subject that carries one of the task's key nouns and confirms something (`confirmation`, `booking`, `receipt`, `Bestätigung`, `Rechnung`, as a word or inside a compound) or that the owner sent, an `event/v1` title that carries a noun for a task that is an appointment (`call`, `meet`, `anrufen`, a `Termin`), repeats the task's verb or carries two nouns, or a `transaction/v1` merchant that carries a noun; the key nouns are the title's content words in English and German (articles, prepositions, pronouns and the verbs of a task set aside), compared case, accents, English plurals and German compounds aside (`Zürich`/`zurich`, `Tromsø`/`tromso`, `flights`/`flight`, `Flug`/`Flugtickets`). By rules alone, no model, no network. Each proposal prints the task, then the evidence's day, time, kind, text, the words shared, `×N sources` when several calendars carry the one entry, and the evidence line's id; a received mail that confirms nothing, a line sharing only the task's verb, a calendar entry sharing one place name with a task that is no appointment, and anything before the task or after the fortnight are not proposed. `done <id>` appends one `task/v1` line and nothing else: `source` `manual`, tier 2, `raw_id` `<source id>@<now>` (the newest snapshot, so `--open` and `--propose-done` leave the task out), the title, `due` and `list` carried over, `status` `done`, `completed_at`, `modified_at`, `supersedes` the snapshot it replaces and `extra.evidence` the line id when one was given; an unknown task or evidence id is refused with status 2, a task already done is left alone. The matcher is a value — a `name`, a `version` and `__call__(task, candidate) -> Evidence | None` — so the promises judge's engine can replace the rules without changing the report, the ids or `done`. `Index.by_id` looks one line up by its id. Synthetic fixtures: a booking confirmation, a German `Buchungsbestätigung`, a call in two calendars, a payment at the marina, and the look-alikes that must not match.
- `logbook sync --all`: every configured live source in turn — configured: at least one of its `ENV` variables set and its `configure` taking the environment — each from its own watermark, a source the owner disabled (`policy/import.json`) or one with no variable set skipped and said so; a failure in one (its exit status, or an error its adapter let through) is said on stderr and never stops the next. Each source prints its summary as it runs; then one line per source (`ok`, `failed (status 1)`, `skipped (LOGBOOK_GRANOLA_KEY not set)`) and exit 1 when any failed. A Ctrl-C while a source listens to a stream (`ais`, status 130) ends the run there, the sources not reached listed as `not run`, status 130. `--dry-run` passes through; `--since`, `--listen` and `--until` are a single source's and refused with `--all`. `logbook sync --install-schedule` / `--uninstall-schedule` (`logbook/schedule.py`, README): `sync --all` at 07:00 and 19:00 local by the machine's own scheduler — a launchd agent on macOS (`~/Library/LaunchAgents/org.logbook.sync.plist`, `StartCalendarInterval`), a systemd user timer on Linux (`~/.config/systemd/user/logbook-sync.{service,timer}`, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME` honoured, `OnCalendar=*-*-* 07,19:00:00`, `Persistent=true`). The plist or units are printed before they are written; nothing is written outside that one directory; the agent runs the interpreter that installed it (`sys.executable -m logbook.cli`) and carries `LOGBOOK_HOME` for the record found at install time; a live source's keys are never written — both read `~/.config/logbook/sync.env` (`KEY=value`, the owner's file) at run time, so the schedule is harmless until the file exists. The scheduler is then told (`launchctl bootstrap`, `systemctl --user enable --now`); when it is not on PATH the command to run is printed; a scheduler that refuses is exit 1 with its words, the files left for a retry. Any other platform exits 2.
- `logbook digest [YYYY-MM-DD] [--json | --markdown]` (`logbook/digest.py`, `docs/digest.md`): the day in 25 lines at most, what the owner reads of it in the evening, composed from the readers and deriving nothing of its own. The Day's shape in three lines — **where** (the stays in order, a run aboard an asset as `aboard <asset>`, and the night after: at home, away or in transit), **with** (the people the Day confirms, and how many are only proposed), **attached** (the counts across the day's rows, and how many lines fell inside no row); the flights of the day, three at most and `+N more`; the open promises due on the day or within seven days after it from the promises reader (`promises.extract`; a report that carries a `judged` set, the ids a judging reader holds to be promises, is narrowed to those — `digest.due_promises`), three at most; the usual sources with no line on the day by the `sources --gaps` rule (`gaps.report` with the day as `since` and the usual sources of the fortnight expected, `days.usual_sources`), one line; tomorrow's timed calendar entries from the `event/v1` lines standing, folded as the Day folds them, with the attendees the record names, three at most; and, last, one closing question the owner can answer in a word, by the first rule that applies — a day with no line asks whether the missing usual source was switched off, else where you were; a night in transit where you slept; a person proposed and confirmed nowhere whether they were with you; a calendar entry inside no row whether it happened; a missing usual source whether it was off; a promise due by the day whether it is done; else `Anything to add?` — so the day closes itself (ADR 0005). The 25 lines are a hard limit (`digest.LIMIT`): the parts are capped and `digest.fit` cuts from the middle, never the header or the question; the JSON holds the whole sets and says how many lines the text has. `--markdown` prints the same lines as a heading, a bulleted list and the question in bold. Nothing is written, not even the settings file; nothing is sent — delivery is a later decision. A day after today in the record's zone is refused.
- `logbook people [--year YYYY] [--json]` and `logbook person <name-or-ref> [--year YYYY] [--json]` (`logbook/people.py`, `docs/people.md`): everyone the record's resolution lines name as a person (RFC 0006), never the owner, and what the record knows of each. Per person: the channels they come through, each with its lines, first and last day, highest tier and last line — `messages` (a `message/v1` they sent, and one the owner sent in a direct chat with them: attributed to the one person who wrote in that chat, else to the person the chat's id names, a WhatsApp JID being a phone number; the owner's lines in a group name nobody), `calls` (answered or not), `mail` (from them or to them, by address), `calendar` (an entry they attend and did not decline, timed or all-day), `transcripts` (a participant, by ref or by a spoken name the record labels one person by), `faces` (a tagged face, through the library's person id); first and last contact over every channel; the days together, the with module's confirmed set (the rules of `trips` and `rollup people`: a tagged face and an all-day entry are proposals, not a day together); the nights under one roof (the nights whose overnight stay the person was confirmed at on that day); the places shared, by days there, labelled as the rollups label a stay; the birthday when a standing resolution line of one of their refs carries `extra.birthday` (`YYYY-MM-DD` or `--MM-DD`); and the last real contact — the latest message, answered call or day together, with how (`message`, `call`, `meeting`); a mail is a channel and never a real contact, a newsletter being a mail too. The tier of a person is the highest tier of their evidence, the resolution lines included; the report's is the highest of its people. `people` is one row per person with evidence in the window, most days together first; `person` is one page — the same numbers, then the shared days, most recent first, each with where, the evidence and `night` when the night was spent under one roof — found by label, a unique first or last name, entity id, address, phone number or `kind:value`; several, nobody or the owner exit 2. The window is the record's days of any kind, or `--year` clipped to them. Read through the index and nothing else: the resolution and retraction lines whole, the owner's stays and nights from the index's columns (`reading.owner_track`), and the six kinds streamed one at a time over the window (`Index.of_kind` now takes local-day bounds), each line read once and kept only when it names someone; a retracted line and one another line of its kind supersedes count for nothing. Synthetic fixtures: the Oslo persona's circle of twelve (`tests/circle.py`).
- `logbook people merge [--propose | --apply ID... | --export-review FILE | --apply-review FILE] [--json]` (`logbook/people_merge.py`, `docs/people.md`): the same person named twice or more by the resolution lines (RFC 0006) — the phone's address book, a Takeout export and a name given by hand to a calendar attendee, a mail sender or a transcript speaker each mint their own entity — proposed with the evidence and never merged on its own. Every person entity, never the owner, is a candidate with every ref that resolves to it through the alias walk; two are one person when a phone meets (the same E.164 number: a `phone` ref as written, one entered without a country code read with `LOGBOOK_DIAL_PREFIX` as the adapters read it, or a WhatsApp JID handle), an email meets (case aside; a `handle` that is an address counts), or the names are one name — the same words in any order, case, accents and punctuation aside, or a first name spelled by its initial, two words at least, never a word one letter off — *and* both are heard on one of `people`'s channels; a name alone is never enough. Matches join into one proposal; the primary is the candidate with the most refs, then the most lines heard, then the one minted first; the id is a digest of the entity ids, stable across runs. `--apply` appends, through `Logbook.append` and nothing else, one `resolution/v1` alias line per ref of a secondary whose own line names it (`alias_of` the primary's ref, a phone before an address; `source` `manual`, `method` `owner`, `supersedes` and `evidence` the lines replaced, `logbook_head`, and under `extra` the proposal id, the two entities and what matched), so the ref and every ref already aliased to it resolve to the primary through the ordinary walk that `people`, `person` and the with module take (a chain that would pass `MAX_HOPS` gets its own line); nothing is retracted, no raw line is touched, and an unknown id exits 2 before anything is written. `--export-review` writes one CSV row per (proposal, secondary) with an empty `apply` column; `--apply-review` merges the rows marked `yes`, says and skips a row already merged, and refuses a row it cannot place before writing. Synthetic fixture: four people duplicated across five sources, with the near misses that stay apart (`tests/duplicates.py`).
- `logbook mcp [--root DIR] [--allow-tier-3]` and `logbook mcp --inspect` (`logbook/mcp_server.py`, the optional extra `openlogbook[mcp]`, `docs/mcp.md`): the record served to an MCP host on this machine over the process's own stdin and stdout, and nothing else — no TCP, no socket. Nine read tools answer as the commands' `--json` does — `day(date)`, `days(from, to)`, `trips(year)`, `places()`, `people()`, `person(name)`, `promises(open)`, `gaps(since)` — and `search(text, since, until, kinds, limit)` finds, through the index, the standing lines of a window of local days and a list of kinds whose payload mentions the text, case aside; two write tools go through `Logbook.append` and nothing else, `add_note(text, at?)` (one `note/v1` line, tier 2, as `logbook add`) and `promise_done(id)` (one `task/v1` line, as `logbook promises done`). Every tool's output passes the crossing gate of ADR 0016: `policy/crossing.json` names an `mcp` destination (`init` writes `{"max_tier": 1}`; a file that does not name it is a ceiling of 1, `policy.mcp_ceiling`, the one destination with a default), read on every call and never written; a line above the ceiling is left out of every reading before anything is derived from it (a `GatedIndex` filters every line, row and aggregate the index serves, so a tier-2 note is in no Day, page, rollup or search at max_tier 1), the answer carries `gate.withheld`, the count of lines left out, and a retraction passes whatever its tier so a retracted line stays hidden. Tier 3 crosses only when the policy allows it and the server was started with `--allow-tier-3`. A read appends no crossing line. `--inspect` prints the tool table and one sample request, with neither a record nor the extra. Tests drive the server through the SDK's in-process client over the synthetic persona; no socket opens.
- `logbook inbox list [--json]` and `logbook inbox clean --to DIR | --delete [--dry-run]` (`logbook/inbox.py`, `adapters/README.md` "Delete after import"): every file under `inbox/` with its size, the import that consumed it and whether every line it produced is in the record; then moving what is imported in full to DIR (an external disk, the path under `inbox/` kept) or removing it, refusing — with its reason — a file nothing imported, one changed since, one imported into another record and one already at DIR, and printing the freed size. A run of `add` (each file) or `import-backup` (each source's folder) that reaches its end now appends one entry to `state/imports.jsonl` — the input, the adapter, lines produced and appended, the `seq` span and its last hash, the record's `owner_id`, the input's size — bookkeeping and never the record; a run that stops early records nothing. A store's `-wal`/`-shm` sibling is taken with its store by name; a backup's `copies.json` and `Manifest.db` go when every other file of their folder has. `add` and `import-backup` end with one line saying the input can be cleaned.
- `logbook add <export> --dry-run` (any file adapter, named or sniffed, a folder or a file; a `.jsonl` of drafts too): runs the adapter and prints `would add N lines from <adapter> (M already there); dry run, nothing written` — the lines `append_many` would write and those it would skip as already in the record by the same `(source, raw_id)` rule, a repeat in the stream counted once, through one query of the index — and what the adapter skipped, as a real run does; nothing is written, not a line, the index or `logbook.json`. A sentence or a declared flight with `--dry-run` exits 2: each is one line, written or not. (`sync --dry-run` already existed; `add` had none.)
- `withings` reads the account's data export (`logbook add withings <folder>`, the CSVs of *Download my data*, or one of them; `logbook add <folder>` recognises it): the scale's standings from `weight.csv`, one `health-sample/v1` line per quantity — `weight`, `fat_mass`, `bone_mass`, `muscle_mass`, `body_water`, and `body_fat_pct` or `bmi` when the export carries them; pounds converted with the entered value under `extra.original` —; the cuff's pulse from `bp.csv` as `heart_rate` with `extra.systolic_mmhg` and `extra.diastolic_mmhg` beside it (blood pressure is no type of the profile, rule 8); each night of `sleep.csv` as an `in_bed` span with the export's totals under `extra`; from the `raw_<device>_<measure>.csv` files the sleep stages as spans, one per run of equal states (0 `awake`, 1 light → `core`, 2 `deep`, 3 `rem`), heart rate capped at one reading a minute per device, and steps, distance and calories earned in quarter-hour UTC buckets per device (rules 2–4), `device` the export's own word (`tracker`, `bed`); each row of `activities.csv` as a `workout` span with the type, steps, calories and distance under `extra`; height, elevation and the `aggregates_*` daily totals counted, never mapped. Columns are found by header, so one the reader does not need may be missing or renamed; a file without one it needs is counted. A naive clock is read in the record's zone; `raw_id` is the instant, so a later export appends nothing. Tier 3. `docs/adapters/health.md` is new: the three health adapters, the exports' files and columns.
- `myfitnesspal` reads the account's CSV export (`logbook add myfitnesspal <Nutrition-Summary-…csv>`, sniffed too): one `energy_intake` line per meal per day, `value` the calories as the export summed them, a span over that local day with `extra.all_day` (rule 10), the meal, every other numeric column slugged (`fat_g`, `carbohydrates_g`, `protein_g`, `sodium_mg`, `saturated_fat` … as the export names them, `water_ml` when it carries water) and the note under `extra`; `raw_id` `energy_intake:<date>:<meal>`. `Date`, `Meal` and `Calories` are needed, a file without them counted; a row without calories or with an unusable date is counted.
- A later export that corrects a sample supersedes the line in the record (RFC 0014 rule 12, new; `logbook/adapters/health_export.py`): `logbook add` hands an adapter whose `run` takes `existing` the record's standing lines by `(source, raw_id)`, batched through the index (`Index.lines_of`, new), and a draft whose sample differs from what stands — value, span, stage, device, unit or `extra` — is written with `raw_id` suffixed `:v2` (`:v3` … when corrected before, the suffix `repair health-units` uses) and `supersedes` the id of the line it replaces, counted `corrected`; one equal to what stands appends nothing. Nothing is rewritten; `health.standing` already follows `supersedes`. `withings` and `myfitnesspal` take it, from their stores and their exports alike.
- `logbook add <file|folder> --dry-run` (and `add <adapter> <path> --dry-run`): the adapter runs, and what would be added, how many of its lines the record already has (the dedupe `append_many` would do, one batched query of the index) and what was skipped are printed; nothing is written, not a line, not a correction. With a sentence or a declared flight it exits 2.
- `logbook sources --gaps [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--expect SOURCE...] [--json]` (`logbook/gaps.py`): where each source went quiet, from the index alone (`Index.activity`: three SELECTs over its columns, one a window query for the longest stretch between consecutive lines; nothing read from the files, however long the log). Per source with lines in the range: the lines, the last line's time, the longest silent stretch (between two lines, from the range's start to the first line under `--since`, or since the last line and still running, `since 2026-09-20 21:00`) and the local days with no line, folded into runs (`2026-09-21..2026-09-30`). The range is each source's first day to today in the record's zone, or `--since`; a line dated after today (a calendar's future entries) is outside it; today is a missing day only once the source has been silent a full day. `--expect` shows only the named sources, a source with no line among them (`no lines`), marks one silent a day or more at any point of the range with `!`, and exits 1 when any is marked, so a cron job can say so. Every line counts, retracted or not. `--since`, `--expect` or `--json` without `--gaps`, a malformed day or one after today exit 2. The plain `sources` listing is unchanged.
- `logbook assets status [--json]` (`logbook/asset_status.py`): per asset of `assets.json`, in registry order, its last known position and how old that fix is — the local time of the fix, `lat,lon` to four decimals, `near <place>, x m|km` for the nearest named place of `places.json` within 5 km (a berth, a harbour, the cabin's jetty), the speed in m/s when the line carries `speed_mps`, and `3 days 4 h ago`; `no fix yet` for an asset the record has never placed. The fix is the asset's standing `location/v1` line with the latest `at` whose `subject` is its id (RFC 0001, ADR 0018), the latest by time, not by order of writing; a retracted or superseded line never counts. Found through the index: `index.sqlite` gains a `subject` column and a `(subject, at)` index (schema 4; an index built by an earlier schema is rebuilt once, by the first reader, as before), so the lookup is one SELECT per asset and one line read from the files, whatever the size of the owner's own track. Under `--json` each asset carries its registry entry and `fix` (`at`, `lat`, `lon`, `speed_mps`, `near {name, m}`, `age_s`, `line`) or `null`. Nothing is written.
- `logbook doctor` (`logbook/doctor.py`): is this machine set up to keep the record? One line per check, `pass`, `warn` or `fail`, a summary, and exit 1 when anything fails; it reads and never writes, not even the empty settings file a reader would create. The checks: the record is found and `verify` is green with `logbook.json` at the head (not found, not intact or `logbook/0.1` is a fail naming what to run); `index.sqlite` is current, read through a read-only connection so a build in progress is never discarded (missing or behind is a warn); `policy/owner.json` is there and names at least one alias (missing or empty a warn, not JSON a fail); `places.json` names a place of kind `home` (none a warn, with the `places add` line); `assets.json` is a registry when it exists; each optional extra, `ais`, `encrypted` and `transcribe`, is installed, found without importing it, with `pip install "openlogbook[<extra>]"` when not; for every live source the record uses — a `state/<name>.json` watermark, or lines of that source when the index is current, never one `policy/import.json` disables — the variables its adapter reads are set, by name only and never a value, and one the adapter refuses says to run `logbook sync <name>` rather than echo it; free space on the record's volume (under 5 GiB a warn, under 512 MiB a fail); and the folder is not one a sync client owns — iCloud Drive (`iCloud Drive`, `Mobile Documents`, `com~apple~CloudDocs`), Dropbox, OneDrive (the folder name or Windows' `OneDrive` variable) or Google Drive — by any part of the path as given or resolved, a fail with the reason. README, "Where to keep the record".
- `logbook sync ais --listen SECONDS` and `--until HH:MM` (`logbook/adapters/ais.py`): one long sitting at the aisstream.io websocket instead of a 60 s window per cron run. `--listen` is the window in seconds, `--until` the record's local time the clock next shows (later today, else tomorrow; a window across the night the clocks change is the hours that pass); without either, `LOGBOOK_AISSTREAM_LISTEN_S` as before. While it listens: one line a minute on stderr with the messages heard, per asset (`8 reports in 120s (solvind 7)`), quiet socket or not; a socket that drops, or a reconnect that fails, is tried again after a wait that doubles from 1 s to 60 s and starts over once a connection delivers, the subscription sent anew each time (said on stderr, counted as `reconnected N times`); a refused key is never retried. Ctrl-C ends the listening, not the run: what was heard is written in time order, the summary printed, and the exit status is 130. The key comes from `LOGBOOK_AISSTREAM_KEY` only and is in no line, notice or error. The flags are refused for a source that does not listen, and `--listen` with `--until`.
- `logbook promises [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--open] [--json]` and `logbook promises done <id> [--note TEXT]` (`logbook/promises.py`, `docs/promises.md`): the commitments the record's own words suggest, proposed and never asserted. One proposal per sentence of a standing `transcript/v1` or `note/v1` line (retracted and superseded lines left out) that carries a first-person future or obligation cue in English or German — `I'll`, `I will`, `I'm going to`, `we'll get back`, `I promise`, `I need to`, `I have to`, `I must`, `let me` (not `let me know`); `ich werde`, `ich schicke`, `ich melde mich`, `ich kümmere mich`, `ich rufe`, `ich muss`, `wir müssen` — not negated right after it (`I will not`, `ich werde nicht`) and not a question; by rules alone, no model, no network. Each carries the local day and time of its line, the origin line id, the speaker when the transcript resolves one (the turn's label matched to `participants`, their refs to a person through the resolution lines, RFC 0006; `me` and the owner's names are `you`, as is every note; `Speaker A` stays as spoken with no person), the sentence as written, and a due hint with a `?` when a date phrase is in it, resolved against the day it was said (a weekday is the next one strictly after; `next week` its Monday; `this week` and `end of the week` the Friday on or after; `tomorrow`, `morgen`, `tonight`, `in two weeks`, `next month`, `by June 20`, `bis 20. Juni`, `bis zum 20.6.`, `the 5th`). A transcript's text is read from the attachment store by media type (WebVTT, SRT, Granola's JSON segments, `Speaker: text` prose, plain text) through the file adapter's parsers; one whose text is not in the store is counted and skipped. The proposal id is a sixteen-hex digest of the origin line and the quoted sentence, so it is the same on every run and for any extractor that quotes the same sentence. `--json` is the report: `extractor`, `skipped`, and every proposal with `id`, `status`, `closed_by`, `day`, `at`, `line`, `seq`, `kind`, `source`, `title`, `speaker`, `direction` (`owed_by_owner`, `owed_to_owner`, null), `certainty` `inferred`, `language`, `cue`, `class`, `quote`, `due`. `done <id>` appends one `task/v1` line (RFC 0016): `source` `manual`, tier 2, the sentence as `title`, `status` `done`, the due hint as `due`, `completed_at`, `list` `promises`, the `--note` as `notes`, `raw_id` `promise:<id>@<time>`, and under `extra` the `promise` id, `origin`, `speaker` and `extractor`; `--open` hides a proposal whose latest standing task line is `done` or `cancelled`; a second `done` writes nothing and says so; an unknown id is refused. The extractor is a value — a `name`, a `version` and `__call__(text) -> list[Match]` — so a later local-model pass replaces the rules without changing the command, the report, the ids or `done`.
- `logbook transcribe voice-memos [--since YYYY-MM-DD] [--model NAME] [--dry-run] [--fetch-model]` (`logbook/transcribe.py`, `docs/adapters/voice-memos.md`): the local transcription step RFC 0023 leaves to later. One `transcript/v1` line (RFC 0004) per standing `voice-memo/v1` line whose audio is in the attachment store and that has no transcript yet, in time order, appended as each is heard, so an interrupted run keeps what it did and the next carries on. The line's `raw_id` is the memo's own, so a memo is transcribed once however often the command runs (ADR 0017); `source` is `transcription` whatever engine heard it; `provider` the engine; `payload.provenance` `{source, engine, model}` with `source` the memo line's id; `extra.recording` the memo's `raw_id`; `language` detected on the first chunk and asked of every later one; the text one segment per line as a `text/plain` attachment, never inline; `at`, `end`, `tz` and `title` the memo's; tier 2, or the memo's when higher. A retracted memo, and one another line supersedes, is left alone, and the transcript never carries `supersedes`. Long recordings are heard in ten-minute windows. Engines, as the optional extra `openlogbook[transcribe]`: `mlx-whisper` on Apple silicon, else `faster-whisper`, found at run time and imported only then; neither installed is one clear message. Nothing leaves the machine: the Hugging Face hub is told it is offline for the length of the run, so no socket is opened (a test refuses every socket while a fake engine hears a two-second synthetic WAV); a model not on this machine stops the command, and `--fetch-model` is the one explicit download. `--dry-run` lists and needs no engine. `--model` is a Whisper size (default `small`) or a repository.
- `logbook demo --days N --seed S --out DIR` (`logbook/demo.py`): a complete synthetic record to try every command on, invented in code and read from nowhere. The Oslo persona, Ines Nordmann, who does not exist, lives a four-week story that repeats for as many days as asked: an office week with a cabin weekend; Zürich for a client, out on a tracked flight (`flighty`, XY 561) and back on one the record infers from its own calendar entry and location points (`flight-inference`, XY 562, written as `logbook infer flights` would, so the command finds it already there and merges the other three); a week aboard the yacht Nordlys with the boat's own AIS track as a subject (`assets.json`, MMSI in the 970xxxxxx test range), so the nights read *aboard* and the trip is an asset trip; Copenhagen, out tracked (XY 571) and back declared (`manual`, XY 572). Around it every profile: location, event, message, transcript (the text in the attachment store), note, photo with favourites and an Art album (keepers follow), call, mail, task, browse, watch, listen, trip, highlight, voice-memo (a digest, no audio), health samples (sleep stages past midnight, steps in quarter hours, resting and ambient heart rate), transaction, and the resolution lines of a circle of twelve fictional people; `places.json` names Home (with its country), Office, Marina and Cabin and leaves the hotels and the anchorages for `places propose`. Phone numbers are in the UK reserved range 07700 900xxx, emails at example.org, the airline is XY, registrations ZZ-. The record is a function of (`days`, `seed`): ids, timestamps, GPS noise, step counts and every text come from one seeded generator and a fixed first day (Monday 1 June 2026), so the same arguments give the same head anywhere. Thirty days is 12,772 lines. `docs/try-it.md`, *Five minutes with a demo record*, walks every reader over it; the README's quick start begins with it; `docs/demo.tape` records it.
- `trips`: a night named by its nearest large airport drops the part after a comma as well as the airport words, so a night off Sandefjord reads `Sandefjord`, not `Sandefjord Airport, Torp`.
- `trips`, "with": the owner is never their own company — `present.owner_of` gathers `owner_id` and `owner_emails` from `logbook.json`, every resolution line that names those, every label they carry, and the record's new `policy/owner.json` (`{"names": [...], "emails": [...], "phones": [...]}`, your other spellings; `init` writes it empty and an older record gets it on the first reading). A calendar attendee counts only for a timed entry held at the stay: its location geocodes within 1 km of the stay's centre (coordinates under `extra.location`, or a `location` naming a place in `places.json`), or it has no location and overlaps the stay by more than an hour; an all-day entry places nobody and a located entry the record cannot place does not count. A transcript participant counts only when the record resolves them to a person (`Speaker A`, `me`, `them`, `Unknown` are nobody). An attendee that does not resolve and has no display name is dropped, and a calendar system address (`calendar.google.com`, `noreply`, `reservations@`, `invite@`) is never a person. At most twelve names, most evidence first. The same rules reach `show place`, `show asset`, `show person` and `rollup people`, which share the with module. (A real record listed the owner, a hotel's reservations address and a diarization label as company.)
- `trips`, the night rule: a night whose stay centre is within 400 m of a place of kind `home` is a night at home whatever that place's radius, so a guest room across the street makes no one-night trip and a trip never starts or ends with such a night. `rollup nights` counts it as home too.
- `trips`, the route: a stay is its named place; else a large airport's name only when the stay is within 2 km of it (it was the nearest within 100 km, so a hotel read as the city); else its coordinates, with `near <place>, x km` for the nearest named place within 5 km. Consecutive route points within 200 m of each other fold into one. `trips.route_of` is the pure function.

### Docs
- `scripts/bench_seal.py` and `docs/rfcs/bench-encryption-2026-10.md`: the benchmark the encryption draft's open question 1 asked for, and its results. A 3,000,000-line synthetic record shaped like the stress tests (1,000,000 tier-1 points, 1,000,000 health-sized tier-3 lines, 1,000,000 message-sized tier-2 lines over ten years of month files) is written plain and sealed per line in the draft's Option C layout (`sealed/v1` reference in `payload`, `payload_enc` beside it, outside the hash) with age X25519 through `pyrage` to one and to two recipients, and with the §9.1 record data key (ChaCha20-Poly1305) for comparison; measured: the seal step per line, opening per line, keyless and keyed verify, index rebuild with opening, bytes on disk per line and in all, and the peak memory of each phase in a subprocess of its own. The script is a pure benchmark run with `uv run --with pyrage python scripts/bench_seal.py` (`--quick` for 300,000 lines); pyrage is not a dependency. `.github/workflows/bench.yml` runs it on the three CI runner sizes by hand only (`workflow_dispatch`), never on push. No product code changes.
- `docs/audit-2026-10.md`: the audit of the repository as of 3 October 2026 — the sizes, the 46 commands, the two adapter registries, which profiles are written and which are read, the duplicate RFC numbers, what the test suite guards and what it pins, what each extra installs — every number from a command in its appendix, and the three decisions the owner took on it (46 commands become 22 with hidden aliases; the read profiles freeze for v1.0, the written-but-unread ones park, `commitment/v1` and `commitment-close/v1` go, `trip/v1` becomes `journey/v1`; one package `openlogbook` in three tiers, core, contrib, labs). On the docs site under Roadmap.
- The repository reads in ten minutes: the README is a landing page (the long form moved to `docs/tour.md`), `docs/index.md` lists every page in one line each, `docs/release-notes.md` says what 0.5 and 0.6 changed for you in five bullets each, SECURITY.md carries the threat model on one page (tiers 2 and 3 are marked, not yet encrypted), CONTRIBUTING.md has one way to do each thing, and the issue templates ask for a synthetic reproduction, an adapter's export and tier, or an RFC's schema and example. The stale version and phase strings are gone.
- RFC 0031, *Profile freeze for v1.0* (`docs/rfcs/0031-profile-freeze.md`, draft): every payload profile sorted by one rule computed from the code into frozen (read by a reader, a rollup or a derive command: 19), parked (written by an adapter, read by nothing: 7) or deleted (`commitment/v1` and `commitment-close/v1`, written and read by nothing: 2), with what each word binds, the `trip/v1` → `journey/v1` rename for new lines with no migration, how a parked profile gets frozen, the unresolved questions and the five pull requests that carry it out. The letter to builders, `docs/letters/2026-10-04-the-freeze.md`, says what the freeze gives, costs and asks. Docs only; nothing in the code or the spec changes until the RFC is accepted.

### Tests
- `tests/test_fuzz_readers.py` (hypothesis): every reader — `show`, `day`, `days`, `trips`, `derive stays --dry-run`, `infer flights --dry-run`, every `rollup`, `places propose`, `keepers`, `sources --gaps`, `promises --all`, `assets status`, `doctor` — runs on `logbook demo` records of any seed and length, in any date window, and exits 0 with well-formed output (valid JSON under `--json`), never a traceback.
